### 1655

<note>This date indicates the book is a copy of the first English translation of "De Occulta Philosophia libri tres," which was originally published in Latin in 1533. The 1655 edition brought Agrippa's complex system of "natural magic" to a broader English-speaking audience during a period of intense interest in the hermetic arts.</note>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page begins the "Preface to the unprejudiced Reader," continuing from the title page (signature A2). It establishes the author's intent to redeem the reputation of Magic from its common association with sorcery.</meta>

# The PREFACE To the unprejudiced Reader.

<image-desc>A large ornamental drop cap letter 'A' featuring elaborate floral motifs and stylized acanthus leaves within a square border.</image-desc>

As the fall of man made himself and all other creatures subject to vanity, so, by reason thereof, the most noble and excellent Arts wherewith the Rational soul was endowed, are by the rusty canker of Time brought unto Corruption. For Magic itself, which the ancients did so divinely contemplate, is scandalized with bearing the badge of all diabolical sorceries: which Art (says *Mirandula* <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494) was an influential Italian Renaissance philosopher who sought to reconcile Hermeticism and Magic with Christian theology.</note>) <note>original Latin: "Pauci intelligunt, multi reprehendunt, & sicut canes ignotos semper allatrant"</note> *Few understand, many condemn, and as dogs always bark at those they know not*: so do many condemn and hate the things they understand not. Many men there are, that abhor the very name and word *Magus*, because of *Simon Magus* <note>A biblical figure (Acts 8:9–24) who attempted to buy the power of the Holy Spirit, often cited as the father of all heretics and false sorcerers.</note>, who being indeed not a *Magus*, but a *Goes* <term>Goes: From the Greek word for "sorcerer" or "wailer," referring to a practitioner of "low" or deceptive magic, often involving the summoning of malevolent spirits, as opposed to the "high" or "natural" magic of the Magus.</term>, that is, familiar with evil Spirits, usurped that Title. But Magic and Witchcraft are far differing Sciences; whereof *Pliny* <margin>Pliny, Book 30 of his *Natural History*</margin> being ignorant, scoffs thereat: for *Nero* (says *Pliny*) who had the most excellent Magicians of the East sent him by *Tiridates* king of *Armenia*, who held that kingdom by him, found the Art after long study and labor altogether ridiculous. Now Witchcraft and Sorcery, are works done merely by the devil, which with respect unto some covenant made with man, he acts by men as his instruments, to accomplish his evil ends: of these, the histories of all ages, people and countries, as also the holy Scriptures, afford us sundry examples.

But *Magus* is a Persian word primitively, whereby

<sig>A 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: is</meta>

<summary>In this preface, the translator defends "Magic" as a noble art that has been unfairly tarnished by its association with witchcraft and demonic sorcery. He argues that true magic is a divine science of the soul, distinct from the deceptive practices of figures like Simon Magus or the failures of Nero.</summary>

<keywords>Magic vs. Witchcraft, Giovanni Pico della Mirandola, Simon Magus, Goes, Sorcery, Pliny the Elder, Emperor Nero, Tiridates of Armenia, definition of Magus</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface from the previous leaf, further refining the definition of "Magic" by distinguishing the high, divine pursuit from its common, darker associations. It focuses on the etymological and historical roots of the word "Magus" across different cultures.</meta>

<header>The Preface.</header>

is expressed as such a one as is altogether conversant in
things divine; and as *Plato* affirmeth, the art of Ma-
gic is the art of worshipping God: and the Per-
sians called their gods *Magos*: hence *Apollonius* <note>Apollonius of Tyana (c. 15–100 AD) was a Greek philosopher and reputed miracle-worker often compared to Jesus in antiquity.</note> saith,
that *Magus* is either a god by nature <note>original Greek: ὁ κατὰ φύσιν Θεὸς</note>, or a servant of the gods <note>original Greek: θεραπευτὴς Θεῶν</note>:
that is, that *Magus* is a name sometimes of him that
is a god by nature, & sometimes of him that is in the
service of God: in which latter sense it is taken in
*Matthew 2:1,2* when the wise men came to worship
Jesus, and this is the first and highest kind, which is
called divine Magic; and these the Latins did en-
title *sapientes*, or wise men: for the fear and worship
of God, is the beginning of knowledge. These wise-
men the Greeks call *Philosophers*; and amongst the E-
gyptians they were termed *Priests*: the Hebrews term-
ed them *Cabalists* <term>Cabalistos: Practitioners of Kabbalah, the Jewish mystical and contemplative system for interpreting the divine nature and the universe.</term>, Prophets, Scribes and Pharisees;
and amongst the Babylonians they were distinguished
by the name of *Chaldeans* <term>Chaldeans: A people of Mesopotamia famous in the ancient world for their mastery of astronomy and astrology.</term>; & by the Persians they were
called *Magicians*: and one speaking of *Sosthenes* <note>Hostanes (or Sosthenes) was a legendary Persian sage believed by the Greeks to have introduced the art of magic to the Western world.</note>, one of
the ancient *Magicians*, useth these words: He honors the true God with the majesty he deserves, and knows how to be present for the worship of the true God alongside his ministering angels; he likewise reveals that there are earthly, wandering demons, enemies of humanity <note>original Latin: "Et verum Deum merita majestate prosequitur, & angelos ministros Dei, sed veri ejus venerationi novit assistere; idem daemonas prodit terrenos, Vagos, humanitatis inimicos"</note>. *Sosthenes* as-
cribeth the due Majesty to the true God, & acknow-
ledgeth that his Angels are ministers and messengers
which attend the worship of the true God; he also
hath delivered, that there are devils earthly and wan-
dering, and enemies to mankind.

So that the word *Magus* of itself imports a Con-
templator of divine & heavenly Sciences; but under
the name of *Magic*, are all unlawful Arts compre-
hended, as Necromancy and Witchcraft, and such
Arts which are effected by combination with the de-
vil, and whereof he is a party.

<meta>catchword: These</meta>

<summary>This page defines the "Magus" as a divine philosopher and servant of God, tracing the term through Persian, Greek, Hebrew, and Babylonian traditions to distinguish "divine Magic" from illicit sorcery.</summary>
<keywords>Magus, divine magic, Plato, Apollonius of Tyana, Sosthenes, Chaldeans, Cabalists, distinction between magic and witchcraft</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface's exploration of the word "Magic," moving from its highest "divine" definition to its common corruption and its secondary branch, Astrology. It provides a historical justification for Astrology by linking it to the biblical patriarch Abraham.</meta>

<header>*The Preface.*</header>
<page-num>10</page-num>

These Witches and Necromancers are also called evil-doers <note>original: *Malefici*</note> or poisoners <note>original: *venefici*</note>; sorcerers or poisoners; by which name witches are rightly called, who without the Art of Magic do indeed use the help of the devil himself to do mischief, practicing the mixing of the powder of dead bodies with other things prepared by the help of the devil; and at other times making images of wax, clay; or otherwise (as it were sacramentally <note>original: *sacramentaliter*; used here to suggest that the witches use physical objects as "unholy sacraments" to enact the devil's will</note>) to bring about those things which the devil accomplishes by other means. Such were, and to this day partly, if not altogether, are the corruptions which have made the very name of Magic hateful, having chiefly sought—as is the way of all frauds—to counterfeit its highest and most noble part.

A second kind of Magic is Astrology, which judges the events of things to come, both natural and human, by the motions and influences of the stars upon these lower elements, as observed and understood by them.

*Philo Judaeus* <note>Philo of Alexandria (c. 20 BCE – 50 CE) was a Jewish philosopher who sought to harmonize the Torah with Greek philosophy.</note> affirms that by this part of Magic or Astrology, together with the motions of the Stars and other heavenly bodies, *Abraham* found out the knowledge of the true God while he lived in *Chaldea*. "He who, through the contemplation of created things, knew the Creator" <note>original: *Qui Contemplatione Creaturarum, cognovit Creatorem*</note> (says *Damascene* <note>John of Damascus (c. 675–749), a Syrian monk and scholar known for his theological writings.</note>). *Josephus* <note>Titus Flavius Josephus (37–c. 100 CE) was a Romano-Jewish historian whose works provide key insights into first-century Judaism.</note> reports of *Abraham* that he instructed the Egyptians in Arithmetic and Astronomy; who, before *Abraham's* coming to them, knew none of these Sciences.

"Abraham, the most outstanding of all in holiness and wisdom, taught first the Chaldeans, then the Phoenicians, and finally the Egyptian priests, Astrology and Divine matters." <note>original: *Abraham sanctitate & sapientia omnium praestantissimus, primum Caldaeos, deinde Phoenices, demum Egyptios Sacerdotes, Astrologia & Divina docuerit.*</note> Abraham the holiest and wisest of men, did first teach the Chalde-

<meta>catchword: ans</meta>

<summary>This page distinguishes between the "corrupt" magic of witches and necromancers and the "scientific" magic of Astrology. It cites historical and religious figures like Philo, Josephus, and Abraham to validate Astrology as a divine pursuit that leads to the knowledge of God.</summary>
<keywords>witchcraft, necromancy, astrology, Abraham, Chaldea, Philo Judaeus, Josephus, John of Damascus, divination, corruption of magic</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the genealogy of sacred knowledge, moving from the biblical patriarchs to the Phoenicians and Egyptians. It defines the "third kind" of Magic—Natural Philosophy or Alchemy—and provides a classic definition of Magic as the art of joining active and passive natural forces. It concludes by presenting Zoroaster as a master of these divine and natural sciences.</meta>

<header>The Preface.</header>

[Chalde]ans, then the *Phoenicians*, and lastly the *Egyptian* Priests, attained Astrology and Divine knowledge.

Without doubt, *Hermes Trismegistus* <note>A legendary Hellenistic figure portrayed as a combination of the Greek god Hermes and the Egyptian god Thoth, credited with writing the foundational texts of Hermeticism.</note>, that divine Magician and Philosopher who (as some say) lived long before *Noah*, attained great Divine knowledge of the Creator through the study of Magic and Astrology; as his writings, which remain with us to this day, testify.

The third kind of Magic contains the entire Philosophy of Nature; it brings to light the innermost virtues <note>In early modern science, "virtues" refers to the inherent powers, properties, or medicinal "essence" of a substance.</note> and extracts them out of Nature's hidden bosom for human use. These are the **"virtues hidden in the very center of the center,"** <note>original: *Virtutes in centro centri latentes*</note> according to the Chemists <note>A term often used interchangeably with Alchemists in this period.</note>. Of this sort were *Albertus* <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a friar and philosopher known for his work in the natural sciences.</note>, *Arnold of Villanova*, *Raymond* <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), a Majorcan philosopher and polymath.</note>, *Bacon* <note>Roger Bacon (c. 1219–1292), an English philosopher and friar who emphasized the study of nature through empiricism.</note>, and others, etc.

The Magic these men professed is defined in this way: **"Magic is the connection, made by a wise man, of natural active agents with passive subjects that correspond to them, so that works may be produced which cause wonder in those who are ignorant of the causes."** <note>original: *Magia est connexio a viro sapiente agentium per naturam cum patientibus sibi, congruenter respondentibus, ut inde opera prodeant, non sine eorum admiratione qui causam ignorant.*</note> Magic is the connection of natural agents and patients <note>In the physics of the time, an "agent" is the force that acts, and a "patient" is the matter that receives the action.</note>, answerable each to the other, wrought by a wise man to bring forth such effects as are wonderful to those who do not know their causes.

In all these matters, *Zoroaster* was well-learned, especially in the first and the highest branch. For in his Oracles, he confesses God to be the first and the highest; he believes in the Trinity, which he would not investigate by any natural knowledge; he speaks of Angels and of Paradise; he approves the immortality of the soul, and teaches Truth, Faith, Hope, and Love, while discussing the abstinence and charity of the *Magi* <note>The priestly caste of ancient Persia, from which the word "magic" is derived.</note>.

<meta>catchword: Of</meta>

<summary>This page defines the "third kind" of magic as Natural Philosophy (Alchemy) and provides a classic definition of Magic as the scientific manipulation of natural forces. It identifies Zoroaster as a supreme practitioner of these arts who also understood Christian-aligned theological truths like the Trinity and the immortality of the soul.</summary>

<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Natural Philosophy, Alchemy, Albertus Magnus, Roger Bacon, Zoroaster, definition of magic, Magi, occult virtues</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on the history of sacred knowledge and the definition of Magic as the perfection of Natural Philosophy. It transitions from the legacy of Hermes Trismegistus to the testimony of Zoroaster, emphasizing that "true" magic is a pious pursuit of divine and natural truths.</meta>

<header>The Preface.</header>

Of this *Zoroaster* <note>The ancient Persian prophet and founder of Zoroastrianism, often regarded by Renaissance scholars as the first and greatest of the "Magi."</note>, *Eusebius* <note>Eusebius of Caesarea (c. 260–339 AD), a historian and bishop known as the "Father of Church History."</note> in his work *The Theology of the Phoenicians*, using *Zoroaster's* own words, says: "He writes these things word for word" <note>original: *Hæc ad verbum ſcribit*</note>: "God is the first, incorruptible, everlasting, unbegotten, without parts, most like unto himself, the guide of all good, expecting no reward, the best, the wisest, the father of right, having learned justice without being taught, perfect by nature, wise, the only inventor of sacred nature, etc." <note>original: *Deus primus, incorruptibilium, ſempiternus, ingenitus, expers partium, ſibiipſi ſimillimus, bonorum omnium auriga, munera non expectans, optimus, prudentiſsimus, pater juris, ſine doctrina juſtitiam perdoctus, natura perfectus, ſapiens, ſacræ naturæ unicus inventor, &c.*</note> Thus says *Zoroaster*, word for word: God the first, incorruptible, everlasting, unbegotten, without parts, most like himself, the guide of all good, expecting no reward, the best, the wisest, the father of right, having learned justice without teaching, perfect, wise by nature, the only inventor thereof.

So that a Magician is nothing else but **"a worshiper and interpreter of divine things"** <note>original: *divinorum cultor & interpres*</note>, a studious observer and expounder of divine things; and the Art itself is none other than **"the absolute perfection of Natural Philosophy"** <note>original: *Naturalis Philoſophiæ abſoluta conſummatio*</note>, then the absolute perfection of Natural Philosophy <note>In the 17th century, "Natural Philosophy" was the study of the physical world and its laws—the precursor to modern science.</note>. Nevertheless, there is a mixture in all things: of good with evil, of falsehood with truth, of corruption with purity. The good, the truth, and the purity in every kind may well be embraced. As in the ancient worshipping of God by Sacrifice, there was no man who knew God among the Elders that refrained from worshipping the God of all power, or condemned that kind of worship, just because the devil was also adored in the images of *Baal*, *Dagon*, *Astaroth*, *Chemosh*, *Jupiter*, *Apollo* <note>A list of ancient deities mentioned in the Bible and Greek mythology, here categorized as "demonic" or false imitations of the true God.</note>, and the like.

Neither did the abuse of *Astrology* terrify *Abraham* (if we believe the most ancient and religious writers) from observing the motions and natures of the heavenly bodies. Neither can it discourage wise and learned

<meta>catchword: men</meta>

<summary>This page defines the "true Magician" as a pious interpreter of nature and divinity, using the testimony of Zoroaster to describe the nature of God. It argues that legitimate sciences like Magic and Astrology should not be abandoned simply because they have been historically abused or associated with idolatry.</summary>
<keywords>Zoroaster, Eusebius, Magician, Natural Philosophy, Theology, Idolatry, Astrology, Abraham, Names of God</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the translator Robert Turner's preface, moving from a definition of the "Magician" as a pious observer of nature to a defense against critics who fear that studying the stars and "Occult Philosophy" is heretical or superstitious.</meta>

# The Preface.

men in these days from attributing those virtues, influences, and inclinations, to the Stars and other Lights of heaven, which God hath given to those his glorious creatures.

I must expect some calumnies <note>False and malicious statements meant to damage someone's reputation.</note> and obtrectations <note>Slanderous detractions or criticisms.</note> against this, from the malicious prejudiced man, and the lazy affecters of Ignorance, of whom this age swarms: but the voice and sound of the Snake and the Goose, is all one. But our stomachs are not now so queasy and tender, after so long time feeding upon solid Divinity, nor are we so shadowy and startling <note>Turner uses "umbragious" (from the Latin *umbra*, shadow) to mean suspicious or easily frightened by shadows.</note>, having been so long enlightened in God's path, that we should relapse into that childish Age, in which *Aristotle's Metaphysics*, in a Council in *France*, was forbidden to be read <note>A reference to the provincial Council of Paris in 1210, which banned the teaching of Aristotle's works on natural philosophy.</note>.

But I incite the Reader to a charitable opinion hereof, with a Christian Protestation of an innocent purpose therein; and entreat the Reader to follow this advice of *Tabaus*, "Those who are at law, let both be in your sight as evil and guilty" <note>original: *Qui litigant, sint ambo in conſpectu tuo mali & rei*</note>. And if there be any scandal in this enterprise of mine, it is taken, not given. And this comfort I have in that Axiome of *Trismegistus* <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom the "Hermetic" wisdom of Egypt was attributed.</note>, "He who is pious, philosophizes in the highest degree" <note>original: *Qui pius est, ſumme philoſophatur*</note>. And therefore I present it without disguise, and object it to all of candor and indifference <note>In this context, "indifference" means impartiality or lack of bias.</note>: and of Readers, of whom there be four sorts, as one observes: Sponges, which attract all without distinguishing; Hour-glasses, which receive, and pour out as fast; Bags, which retain only the dregs of Spices, and let the Wine escape; and Sieves, which retain the best only. Some there are of the last sort, and to them I present this *Occult Philosophy*, knowing that they may reap good thereby. And they who are severe against it, they shall pardon this my opinion, that such their severity proceeds from Self-guiltiness; and give me leave to apply that of *Ennodius* <note>Magnus Felix Ennodius (c. 473–521 AD), a Bishop and Latin rhetorician.</note>, that it is the nature of Self-wickedness, to think that of others, which themselves deserve. And it is all the comfort which the guilty have, Not to find any innocent. But that amongst others this may find some acceptation, is the desire of

->*London, last of August 1654.* *R. Turner.*<-

<summary>In this concluding section of the preface, Robert Turner defends the publication of "Occult Philosophy" against potential critics, arguing that true piety leads to higher philosophy and categorizing readers into four types based on their ability to discern truth.</summary>
<keywords>Robert Turner, Occult Philosophy, Hermes Trismegistus, Aristotle, censorship, 17th-century magic, astrology, Ennodius</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page transitions from Robert Turner’s own preface to a commendatory poem written by a friend or colleague (identified by the initials I.P.B.). Such poems were standard in 17th-century books, serving as "blurbs" to praise the translator's skill and the importance of the work.</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative horizontal header consisting of two rows of repeating typographic fleurons (ornamental floral stamps).</image-desc>

# To his special friend Mr. R. Turner, on
# his wise Translation of
# Corn. *Agrippa*.

Just like someone who has only just emerged from a trance,
Stunned by strange visions, whose secret fears
Have barely passed, but who—doubting whether he
Can trust his own eyes—remains fixed
Steadfastly upon those objects; just like him, I stand
Lost in amazement to behold a man who can,
Through the magical arts, draw near to the gods, and far surpass
The Angels who dwell in those shining heavenly spheres.
Behold Agrippa climbing the high heavens,
Conversing with the gods; and then, in an instant, he peers
Into the deep hidden treasury <note>The original uses "cabinet," a term often used in the 17th century to describe a collection of secrets or a private room for study.</note> of the earth. Just as they are to Mercury <note>The Roman messenger god, associated with the Greek Hermes (Hermes Trismegistus), the legendary founder of magic and alchemy.</note>,
All kinds of spirits are his willing subjects.
His book provides even more than this—but we
Are blind mortals, and could never be led to see
That light without a candle: therefore, to us,
You must be both a new Agrippa and an Oedipus <note>In Greek mythology, Oedipus was the hero who solved the riddle of the Sphinx; the poet is suggesting that Turner has solved the "riddle" of Agrippa’s difficult text for the English public.</note>.
Agrippa appears once again, thanks to you,
Rescued from the ashes of ancient times.
Let squint-eyed Envy wither away, while you
Wear crowns of praise upon your deserving brow.

->I.P.B. Cambridge <note>original: "Cantabrigiæ," the Latin name for Cambridge.</note><-

<summary>This page contains a commendatory poem by an anonymous scholar at Cambridge praising Robert Turner for translating Agrippa's work, comparing Turner to Oedipus for deciphering ancient mysteries.</summary>
<keywords>Robert Turner, Cornelius Agrippa, occult philosophy, Mercury, Oedipus, Cambridge, commendatory verse, translation</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the series of commendatory poems preceding Robert Turner’s translation of Henry Cornelius Agrippa’s works. While the previous poem was signed by "I.P.B.," this one is attributed to a member of St. John’s College, Cambridge. These verses serve to legitimize the work by distinguishing "Natural Magic" from "Necromancy."</meta>

<image-desc>A horizontal row of decorative printer's fleurons (ornamental patterns) across the top of the page.</image-desc>

## To his ingenious friend Mr. Turner, upon his Translation.

*Thrice-noble Soul! renowned epitome*
*Of Learning and Occult Philosophy;*
*Who imparts that unknown Geomancy <note>A method of divination that interprets markings on the ground or the patterns formed by tossed handfuls of soil or rocks.</note>,*
*With the profound secrets of that obscure Art!*
*To explain Natural Magic <note>In the 17th century, "Natural Magic" referred to the study of the hidden powers of nature (like magnetism or herbs), which was considered scientifically and religiously acceptable.</note> is your task;*
*Not to unmask hell-born Necromancy <note>The forbidden practice of communicating with the spirits of the dead.</note>;*
*Exposing Mysteries to public view,*
*That previously were known to very few.*
*You do not keep your Knowledge to yourself,*
*(As base, covetous Misers do their wealth <note>original: "pelf"</note>;*
*Whose numerous bags of rust-eaten gold,*
*Benefit no one, until the owners themselves are laid in the earth)*
*But, studious of the Public good, you make*
*All people partake of the fruits of your labors.*
*Therefore if some picky critic blames*
*Your Writings, surely then his judgment is flawed.*
*Art has no hater but the ignorant <note>original: "empty pate," a slang term for a person lacking intelligence.</note>,*
*Which can far better complain than imitate.*
*Indeed, Zoilus or Momus <note>Zoilus was a Greek grammarian known for his harsh criticism of Homer; Momus was the personification of mockery and unfair criticism in Greek mythology.</note> will not dare*
*To blame your Translation, which is beyond compare*
*Excellent. So that if Mother Nature had bestowed*
*A hundred tongues, and lungs made of brass;*
*Yet to rightly proclaim <note>original: "ebuccinate," from the Latin *buccinare*, meaning to sound a trumpet.</note> your praises,*
*I should lack the strength, as well as the polished phrases.*
*But if the gods will grant what I do crave,*
*Then Enoch's Translation <note>A scholarly pun. In the Bible, Enoch was "translated" (taken directly to heaven without dying). The author wishes Turner both eternal life and success for his book translation.</note> shall you have.*

->**W. P., St. John's College, Cambridge.**<-

<summary>This page contains a commendatory poem by a Cambridge scholar praising Robert Turner for making the "secrets" of natural magic accessible to the public. It emphasizes that Turner's work is a service to the "Public good" rather than a pursuit of dark or forbidden arts.</summary>
<keywords>Robert Turner, Natural Magic, Geomancy, Necromancy, Commendatory Poem, St. John's College Cambridge, Occult Philosophy, Zoilus, Momus, Enoch's Translation</keywords>

## To his friend the Author, on this his
# Translation.

*What, not a Sibyl <note>Prophetesses in ancient Greece and Rome who were believed to possess divine knowledge of the future.</note> or Cassandra <note>A figure in Greek mythology cursed to utter true prophecies that were never believed.</note> left?*
*Apollo <note>The Greek and Roman god of prophecy, music, and healing.</note> ceas'd? Has sharp-fang'd Time bereft*
*Us of the Oracles? Is Dodan's grove <note>The grove of Dodona, the oldest oracle in ancient Greece, where priests interpreted the rustling of oak leaves to understand the will of Zeus.</note>*
*Cut down? Does ne'er a word proceed from Jove <note>Another name for Jupiter, the king of the Roman gods.</note>*
*Into the ears of mortals that inherit*
*Tiresias <note>A famous blind prophet of Thebes in Greek mythology.</note> soul, or the great Calcha's <note>Calchas, the powerful seer for the Greek fleet during the Trojan War.</note> spirit?*
*What is become o' th' Augurs <note>Ancient Roman religious officials who interpreted the will of the gods by observing the flight of birds.</note> that foretold*
*Nature's intents? Are th' Magi <note>Originally Zoroastrian priests from ancient Persia, later associated with wisdom and astrological knowledge.</note> dead, that could*
*Tell what was done in every Sphere? Shall we*
*Not know what's done in the remot'st Country*
*Without great travel? Can't we belowe descry*
*The minde o' th' gods above? All's done by thee,*
Agrippa; <note>Henry Cornelius Agrippa (1486–1535), the original author of the work being translated.</note> *all their Arts lie couch'd in thee.*
*Th' Art that before in divers heads did lie,*
*Is now collect int' one Monopoly.*
*But all's in vain, we lack'd an Oedipus <note>The Greek hero who solved the riddle of the Sphinx; the poet uses him as a metaphor for a brilliant interpreter who can solve difficult mysteries.</note>,*
*Who should interpret's meaning unto us:*
*This thou effect'st with such dexterity,*
*Adding perhaps what th' Author ne'er did see;*
*That we may say, Thou dost the Art renew:*
*To thee the greater half of th' praise is due.*

->*J. B. Cambridge <note>original: "Cantabrigia," the Latin name for Cambridge.</note>.*<-

# To the Author, on his Translation of
# Cornelius Agrippa.

*You are the Pallas <note>Pallas Athena, the Greek goddess of wisdom, crafts, and strategic war.</note> of Learning, if a Goddess may be named;*
*Whose knowledge has explained the original prototype;*
*Which Nature also, striving to combine*
*Science and Learning in this form of yours,*
*Shows to us not darkly, but quite clearly:*
*The knowledge of Mysteries is enshrined in you.*
*By your permission, we have access*
*To <term>Geomancy</term> <note>A method of divination that interprets markings on the ground or the patterns formed by tossed soil, rocks, or sand.</note>, which, unless*
*You had unmasked it, would have remained a mystery,*
*A task too hard for mortals to explain.*
*Since you have preserved this from the Lethæan <note>Referring to the Lethe, the river of forgetfulness in the Greek underworld; used here to mean the depths of oblivion.</note> floods,*
*We will consecrate the laurel buds*
*To you: (dismissing Phœbus <note>An epithet for Apollo, the god of prophecy and the sun.</note>) yours shall be*
*The Oracle <note>A person or place through which divine advice or prophecy was sought.</note>, to which all men shall flee*
*In times of danger; your predictions shall*
*Enthrall our willing hearts to whatever you command;*
*Yes, you shall be the*
*Sole Prophet <note>A teacher or proclaimer of the will of God or the gods.</note>, and we shall be obedient to you.*

->J. R.<-

## *To the Author, on his Translation of*
## Cornelius Agrippa.

*Does Phœbus <note>original: "Phœbus"—An epithet for Apollo, the Greek and Roman god of the sun, prophecy, and the arts.</note> cease to answer our demands?*
*Or will he not accept from mortal hands*
*A humble* <term>Bidental</term>? <note>In ancient Rome, a sacred place struck by lightning, consecrated by the sacrifice of a sheep. Here, it refers to a sacred or propitiatory offering.</note> *And is the* Sibyl’s <note>Ancient prophetesses who spoke the oracles of the gods.</note> *cave*
*Uninhabitable? Or may* Tiresias <note>The famous blind prophet of Thebes in Greek mythology.</note> *have*
*No successor nor rival? How shall we*
*Then direct* Oedipus <note>The mythological king of Thebes famed for solving the riddle of the Sphinx; he is used here as a symbol of someone who can solve deep mysteries.</note> *to the world? If he*
*Adds* Incest *to* Parricide <note>The killing of a parent or near relative.</note>, *they are silent,*
*Who could predict what things would surely come:*
*And they are silent who knew when to apply*
*To our* Body Politic <note>A metaphor describing a nation or state as if it were a human body.</note> *a* Purge *and* <term>Phlebotomy</term>. <note>The practice of bloodletting, used here as a metaphor for "curing" the problems of the state.</note>
*How will bold thieves rob our treasures, or who shall*
*Regain lost goods, or by his* Charms *recall*
*The* <insert>guilty</insert>? <note>original: "nocent"—an archaic term for someone who is harmful or guilty.</note> *The* Art *is by you rescued:*
*In you the* Magi <note>Ancient wise men or practitioners of secret arts.</note> *seem to be revived.*
Phœbus *is not sick in the mind,* Jove's *doves are not dead,*
*The* Oracles *have not ceased: and* Agrippa's *bed*
*(Like the* Arabian bird's <note>The Phoenix, a mythical bird that lived for centuries, burned itself on a funeral pyre, and was then reborn from its own ashes.</note> *self-built nest,*
*Which first serves as her* Urn <note>A vessel for holding the ashes of the dead.</note>, *then her place of rebirth)*
*Has produced you, who are surely more than his equal,*
*Otherwise this* Art *would have remained obscure,*
*A miracle to common people, well known to none,*
*And hardly understood by those of the deepest intellect.*
*Then I will conclude: Since you explain him so well,*
*That the younger* brother <note>Referring to the translator as the "younger brother" to the original author, Agrippa.</note> *has the better* brain.

->John Tomlinson,<-
->of St. John's College in Cambridge.<-

## To his good friend the Author, on his
## Translation of *Occult Philosophy*
## and <term>Geomancy</term>. <note>A method of divination that interprets markings on the ground or the patterns formed by tossed handfuls of soil, rocks, or sand.</note>

Most noble undertakings! As if **Art**
And **Prudence** <note>In this context, practical wisdom or foresight.</note> should a bargain make, to share
Brilliant light: you send forth a ray
Which even the noblest Patrons could never display.
Well may **Diana** <note>The Roman goddess of the moon and nature, often associated with hidden or "occult" mysteries.</note> love you, and inspire
Your noblest **Genius** <note>Referring to one's innate spirit or guiding talent.</note> with **celestial fire**,
Whose sparkling **Fancy** <note>Imagination or creative power.</note> with more power can subdue,
And sooner conquer, than a **Magic Spell**.
The Author <note>Refers to the original writer, Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa.</note> thought not (when he wrote the Book)
To be surpassed by a more profound look,
Or be overtaken by a more triumphant style,
Which should exalt his then-most pleasant vein.
But seeing that a later generation
Has snatched his honor from obscurity,
Both shall revive, and make Spectators know
The best deservers of the **laurel wreath**. <note>original: "Lawrel bow"—A symbol of victory, honor, and poetic excellence.</note>
**Nature** and **Art** here strive for the victory:
And though he refuses to yield,
You have gotten the start: though he triumphs in praise,
Yet may his **Ivy** <note>A plant associated with immortality and the scholar’s life.</note> wait upon your **Bay leaves**. <note>original: "Bays"—symbolizing the highest award for poetic or intellectual achievement.</note>

->M. S.<-
->Cambridge <note>original: "Cantabrigiæ"—The Latin name for the city and University of Cambridge.</note><-

# To the Author, on this his ingenious
### Translation of
# Cornelius Agrippa.

> What is it I see? Agrippa made to wear
> An **English habit**? <note>A "habit" refers to clothing or attire; the poet is using the metaphor of dressing the author in new clothes to describe the act of translation into English.</note> Surely it is something rare.
> Or are his **Romane garments**, <note>A reference to the original Latin in which Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa wrote his works.</note> by your **Wit**,
> Translated to an English garb so fit
> To illustrate him? For we see that you have
> Enlightened his obscure **Philosophie**;
> And that which remained so **intricate**,
> You have exposed to every **vulgar brain**. <note>original: "vulgar brain"—In the 17th century, "vulgar" referred to the "common" people or the general public who did not have a classical education in Latin.</note>
> If then your beams shine so clearly through such dark works,
> How **splendent** <note>Brilliant or shining magnificently.</note> will they appear in your own!
> Then go you on, brave soul, to spread such rays
> Of learning through the world, which may speak your praise.
> And fear no **Criticks**: for you, by a **Spell**,
> Can force their tongues to stay within their teeth. <note>An idiomatic way of saying the critics will be silenced or unable to speak against the quality of the work.</note>

->John Tabor,<-
->of St. John’s College in Cambridge.<-

<meta>This page begins the formal treatise on Geomancy by Henry Cornelius Agrippa, moving from the introductory commendations seen on previous pages into the technical definition of the art. The text establishes the elemental and spiritual foundations of the practice, specifically its connection to the Earth.</meta>

<image-desc>Two horizontal decorative woodcut bands at the top of the page. The upper band is composed of a row of stylized floral or thistle-like motifs. The lower band is a larger rectangular panel containing a dense, symmetrical pattern of interlocking foliate scrollwork and arabesques.</image-desc>

# Henry Cornelius Agrippa,
## *of Geomancy.*

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut historiated initial "G" framing a seated figure of a bearded man, likely a philosopher or sage, who is pointing toward a book or tablet.</image-desc>Geomancy is an art of divination <note>The practice of seeking knowledge of the future or the unknown by supernatural means.</note> by which a judgment may be rendered by lot <note>The use of random objects or chance, like rolling dice or drawing lines, to determine an outcome.</note> or destiny for any question on any subject. The core of this art consists specifically of certain points from which figures are derived, following the rules of equality or inequality, likeness or unlikeness. These figures are then linked to celestial configurations <note>Refers to the positions of stars and planets in the sky, as used in astrology.</note>, taking on their natures and properties according to the movement and forms of the Signs and Planets.

However, we must first consider that this kind of art can reveal nothing of the truth unless it is rooted in some higher power <note>original: "radical in some sublime vertue"</note>. The authors of this science have demonstrated this to be two-fold: the first part consists of religion and ceremonies. Therefore, they require the 'projecting' <note>The act of marking or casting points on a surface, usually the ground or paper.</note> of the points of this art to be made with signs in the Earth. For this reason, the art is assigned to the element of Earth, just as pyromancy <term>Pyromancy: Divination by observing fire or flames.</term> is assigned to Fire, and hydromancy <term>Hydromancy: Divination through the observation of water.</term> is assigned to the element of Water.

Secondly, they believed that the hand of the practitioner <note>original: "Projector or Worker"</note> is most powerfully moved and directed by terrestrial spirits; therefore, they first used certain holy incantations <note>Spells or charms spoken or sung during a ritual.</note> and <note>The text continues on the next page with "deprecations," referring to prayers meant to ward off evil.</note>

***

<image-desc>A horizontal row of nine decorative fleurons (stylized floral ornaments) across the top of the page.</image-desc>

### To the Reader on this his translation of
## Cornelius Agrippa.

<unclear>While others with more than common effort,</unclear>
<unclear>Search for gold in the Peruvian veins;</unclear> <note>A reference to the Spanish silver and gold mines in South America, then a symbol of ultimate worldly wealth.</note>
<unclear>Your more talented Muse a better mine has found,</unclear>
<unclear>Whose natural brightness is with knowledge crowned:</unclear>
<unclear>Let others boast of wealth and worldly store,</unclear>
<unclear>Your book is a treasure, and your mind is even more.</unclear>
<unclear>In this, the various stages of life we view,</unclear>
<unclear>What is false we are taught, and what we find is true. </unclear>
<unclear>You have the skill to make even folly wise,</unclear>
<unclear>And show where error’s greatest pride exists. </unclear>
<unclear>Then let the world your worth and labors prize,</unclear>
<unclear>Since by your efforts we are taught to be more wise.</unclear>

->J. Tabor.<-
->of the University of Cambridge.<-

It establishes that the "truth" of the divination relies on the focused intent of the person asking the question.</meta>

# Henry Cornelius Agrippa,

prayers <note>original: "deprecations," referring to formal entreaties or rhythmic prayers intended to avert evil or invite spiritual presence</note>, along with other rites and observations, which please and attract spirits of this nature to this work.

There is another power that directs and rules this "Lot" or Fate, which resides in the very soul of the practitioner <term>Projector|The person performing the divination by 'projecting' or marking points on a surface</term>. This occurs when they are moved to this work by a great outpouring <note>original: "egresse"</note> of their own desire. This Art possesses a natural obedience to the soul itself; by necessity, it is effective and is moved toward that which the soul desires. This method is by far the more accurate and pure, and it does not matter where or how these points are cast. 

Therefore, this Art shares the same root <note>original: "Radix"</note> as the Art of Astrological Questions <note>Also known as Horary Astrology, a system where a chart is cast to answer a specific question based on the moment it is asked.</note>. This, too, cannot be confirmed or made true except through the steady and intense focus <note>original: "excessive affection"</note> of the seeker <term>Querent|The person asking the question and seeking an answer from the oracle</term> themselves. 

Now, so that we may proceed to the practice <note>original: "Praxis"</note> of this Art: first, it must be understood that all the Figures upon which this entire Art is based number only sixteen. These are noted with their names in the following Table:

# Regarding <term>Geomancy|A method of divination that interprets patterns of dots. The word comes from the Greek 'geōmanteía,' meaning 'earth-divination,' as marks were traditionally made in the soil.</term>

| | | |

<meta>This page continues the technical breakdown of geomantic figures, moving from the visual table on the previous page to the specific astrological correspondences (planets and zodiac signs) that govern their interpretation.</meta>

### Henry Cornelius Agrippa

Now we proceed to explain with which **Planets** these figures are distributed; for upon this depends all the specific characteristics and natures of the figures, as well as the judgment of the entire art. Therefore, the <term>Greater Fortune|A figure representing significant success, stability, and inner strength.</term> <note>original: "Fortuna Major"</note> and <term>Lesser Fortune|A figure representing outward success or quick, temporary gains.</term> <note>original: "Fortuna Minor"</note> are attributed to the **Sun**; however, the first or **Greater Fortune** occurs when the Sun is <term>diurnal|Active during the day; planets were traditionally thought to behave differently depending on whether the sun was above or below the horizon.</term> and positioned in its <term>dignities|Positions in the zodiac where a planet is particularly strong or "at home."</term>; the other, or **Lesser Fortune**, occurs when the Sun is <term>nocturnal|Active during the night.</term> or placed in lesser dignities. **The Way** <note>original: "Via"</note> and **The People** <note>original: "Populus"</note> are referred to the **Moon**; the first from her beginning and increasing phases, the second from her full light and the decreasing quarter. **Gain** <note>original: "Acquisitio"</note> and **Joy** <note>original: "Letitia"</note> belong to **Jupiter**. However, the first has Jupiter as the greater fortune, the second as the lesser, but without any <term>detriment|A state of astrological weakness.</term>. **The Maiden** <note>original: "Puella"</note> and **Loss** <note>original: "Amissio"</note> belong to **Venus**; the first is fortunate, the other is, as it were, <term>retrograde|Appearing to move backward in the sky, often signifying reversals.</term> or <term>combust|An astrological term for a planet being so close to the Sun that its influence is "burnt up" or hidden.</term>. **Union** <note>original: "Conjunctio"</note> and **White** <note>original: "Albus"</note> are both figures of **Mercury**, and both are good, though the first is more fortunate. **The Boy** <note>original: "Puer"</note> and **Red** <note>original: "Rubens"</note> are figures attributed to **Mars**; in the first, Mars is benevolent, in the second, malevolent. **The Prison** <note>original: "Carcer"</note> and **Sorrow** <note>original: "Tristitia"</note> are both figures of **Saturn**, and both are evil; but the first causes the greater harm. The **Dragon’s Head** <note>original: "Caput Draconis"</note> and **Dragon’s Tail** <note>original: "Cauda Draconis"</note> follow their own unique natures.

And these are the infallible comparisons of the figures, and from these we may easily distinguish the equality of their signs. Therefore, the **Greater** and **Lesser Fortunes** have the sign of **Leo**, which is the House of the **Sun**. **The Way** and **The People** have the sign of **Cancer**, which is the House of the **Moon**. **Gain** has for its sign **Pisces**, and **Joy** has **Sagittarius**, which are both the Houses of **Jupiter**. **The Maiden** has the sign of **Taurus**, and **Loss** has **Libra**, which are the Houses of **Venus**. **Union** has for its sign **Virgo**, and **White** the sign of **Gemini**, the Houses of **Mercury**. **The Boy** <note>The source text here uses "Puella" (The Maiden), likely a scribal or printing error, as the context of Mars and the previous paragraph identifies this as "Puer" (The Boy).</note> and **Red** have for their sign **Scorpio**, the House of **Mars**. **The Prison** has the sign of **Capricorn**, and **Sorrow** has **Aquarius**, the Houses of **Saturn**. The **Dragon’s Head** and **Tail** are divided thus: the **Head** to **Capricorn**, and the **Dragon’s Tail** is attached to **Scorpio**. From this, you may easily determine the <term>triplicities|Groups of three zodiac signs belonging to the same element: Fire, Earth, Air, or Water.</term> of these signs, following the pattern of the triplicities of the signs of the **Zodiac**. **The Boy**, therefore, along with both **Fortunes** and **Joy**, govern the **fiery triplicity**; **The Maiden**, **Union**, **The Prison**, and the **Dragon’s Head** govern the

<meta>catchword: earthly</meta>

<meta>This page continues the technical classification of geomantic figures, moving from their planetary associations on the previous page to their elemental groupings (triplicities) and their specific assignments to the twelve signs of the Zodiac. It concludes by introducing the practical method of generating the initial figures of a divination.</meta>

->of Geomancy.<-

an earthly triplicity : **White** <note>original: "Albus"</note>, **Friendship** <note>original: "Amitia", likely a variant of Amissio (Loss) or Amicitia (Friendship)</note>, and **Sorrow** <note>original: "Tristitia"</note>, do make the Airy triplicity : and **The Way** <note>original: "Via"</note>, **The People** <note>original: "Populus"</note>, and **The Red** <note>original: "Rubeus"</note>, with the **Dragon’s Tail**, and **Gain** <note>original: "Acquisitio"</note> do rule the watery triplicity, and this order is taken according to the course or manner of the signs.

But if any one will constitute these <term>triplicities|Groups of three signs or figures belonging to the same element (Fire, Air, Earth, or Water).</term> according to the natures of the **Planets**, and **Figures** themselves, let him observe this Rule, that **Greater Fortune** <note>original: "Fortuna major"</note>, **The Red** <note>original: "Rubeus"</note>, **The Boy** <note>original: "Puer"</note>, and **Loss** <note>original: "Amissio"</note> do make the fiery triplicity : **Lesser Fortune** <note>original: "Fortuna minor"</note>, **The Girl** <note>original: "Puella"</note>, **Joy** <note>original: "Lætitia"</note>, and **Union** <note>original: "Conjunctio"</note> triplicity of the **Air** : **Gain** <note>original: "Acquisitio"</note>, the **Dragon’s Tail**, **The Way** <note>original: "Via"</note>, and **The People** <note>original: "Populus"</note> do govern the watery triplicity ; and the earthly triplicity is ruled by **The Prison** <note>original: "Carcer"</note>, **Sorrow** <note>original: "Tristitia"</note>, **White** <note>original: "Albus"</note>, and the **Dragon’s Head**. And this way is rather to be observed than the first which we have set forth ; because it is constituted according to the Rule and manner of the signs.

This order is also far more true and rational than that which is commonly used, which is described after this manner : of the **Fiery triplicity** are, the **Tail** <note>original: "Cauda"</note>, **Lesser Fortune**, **Loss**, and **The Red** : of the **Airy triplicity** are, **Gain**, **Joy**, **The Boy**, and **Union** : of the **watery triplicity** are, **The People**, **The Way**, **White**, and **The Girl** : And the **Head** <note>original: "Caput"</note>, **Greater Fortune**, **The Prison**, and **Sorrow** are of the earthly triplicity.

They do likewise distribute these Figures to the twelve signs of the **Zodiac**, after this manner, **Gain** is given to **Aries** ; **Fortune**, both **greater** and **lesser** to **Taurus** ; **Joy** to the sign **Gemini** ; **The Girl** and **The Red** to **Cancer** ; **White** is assigned to **Leo**, **The Way** to **Virgo** ; the **Dragon’s Head**, and **Union** to **Libra** ; **The Boy** is submitted to **Scorpio** ; **Sorrow** and **Loss** are assigned to **Sagittarius** ; the **Dragon’s Tail** to **Capricorn** ; **The People** to **Aquarius** ; and **The Prison** is assigned the sign **Pisces**.

And now we come to speak of the manner of <term>projecting|The act of generating geomantic points, usually by making random marks in the sand or on paper.</term> or setting down these Figures, which is thus ; that we set down the points according to their course in four lines, from the right hand towards the left, and this in four courses : There will therefore result unto us four Figures made in four several lines, according to the even or uneven marking every several line ; which four Figures are wont to be called **Mothers** <note>original: "Matres"</note> : which do bring forth the rest, filling up and completing the whole Figure of Judgment.

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for geomancy. Having established the first four primary figures (the Mothers) and their derivatives (the Daughters) on the previous page, Agrippa now explains how these eight figures are used to populate the twelve "Houses" of a geomantic chart and how to calculate the final "Judge" of the divination.</meta>

# Of Geomancy.

And these eight figures make eight <term>Houses of Heaven|Divisions of the geomantic or astrological chart representing different areas of life, such as wealth, health, or travel.</term>, in this manner: by placing the figures from the left hand toward the right. Just as the four **Mothers** <note>original: "Matres"</note> make the first four Houses, so the four **Daughters** <note>original: "Filiæ"</note> make the four following Houses—which are the fifth, sixth, seventh, and eighth. The rest of the Houses are found in this way: the ninth is derived from the first and second; the tenth from the third and fourth; the eleventh from the fifth and sixth; and the twelfth from the seventh and eighth. This is done by the combination or joining together of two figures, following the rule of even or uneven numbers in the remaining points of each figure <note>A mathematical process where two figures are merged: two dots (even) plus one dot (uneven) results in one dot (uneven); two plus two results in two; one plus one results in two.</note>. In the same manner, from the last four figures—that is, the ninth, tenth, eleventh, and twelfth—two figures are produced which they call **Helpers** <note>original: "Coadjutrices"</note> or **Witnesses** <note>original: "Testes"</note>. From these two, one final figure is formed, which is called the **Index** <note>original: "Index"; often called the Judge</note> of the whole chart, or of the matter <term>quesited|The subject or question being asked about.</term>, as appears in the following example.

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for geomancy. Having established the first four primary figures (the Mothers) and their derivatives (the Daughters) on the previous page, Agrippa now explains how these eight figures are used to populate the twelve "Houses" of a geomantic chart and how to calculate the final "Judge" of the divination.</meta>

<page-num>8</page-num>
<header>Henry Cornelius Agrippa,</header>

And what we have just described is the common method observed by geomancers, which we neither entirely reject nor highly praise; therefore, this must also be considered in our judgments. Now, therefore, I shall give to you the true Figure of <term>Geomancy|A method of divination that interprets patterns of dots to reveal hidden knowledge.</term>, according to the correct structure of astrological reasoning, which is as follows:

As the first **Mothers** <note>original: "Matres"</note> make the four <term>Angles|In astrology, these are the four most powerful houses in a chart: the 1st, 4th, 7th, and 10th.</term> of a House—the first making the first Angle, the second making the second Angle, the third making the third Angle, and the fourth making the fourth Angle—so the four **Daughters** <note>original: "Filiae"</note> arising from the Mothers constitute the four <term>succedent Houses|The houses that follow the Angles: the 2nd, 5th, 8th, and 11th.</term>. The first Daughter makes the second House, the second makes the eleventh, the third makes the eighth, and the fourth makes the fifth house <note>The original text says "first House" here, likely a printer's error for the 5th House, given the astrological logic being described.</note>. The rest of the Houses, which are <term>Cadents|The houses that "fall away" from the angles: the 3rd, 6th, 9th, and 12th. They are generally considered weaker positions.</term>, are to be calculated according to the rule of their <term>triplicity|A grouping of three, often relating to the four elements (Fire, Earth, Air, Water) in astrology.</term>; that is to say, by creating the ninth House out of the fourth and fifth, the sixth House out of the tenth and second, the third House out of the seventh and eleventh, and the twelfth House out of the fourth and eighth.

And now you have the entire Figure of true judgment, constructed according to true and effective reasoning, by which I shall show you how to complete it. The figure that falls in the first House shall give you the <term>ascending sign|The zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon at the moment of the question.</term> which the first figure represents. Once this is done, you shall assign the other zodiac signs to the rest of the Houses in their proper order. Then, in every House, you shall note the planets according to the nature of the figure. From all of these elements, you shall build your judgment according to the meaning of the planets in the signs and Houses where they are found, according to their <term>aspects|The geometric angles between planets, believed to influence their relationship (e.g., harmony or conflict).</term> among themselves, and to the position of the <term>querent|The person asking the question.</term> and the thing <term>quesited|The person, object, or outcome being asked about.</term>. You shall judge according to the nature of the signs ascending in their Houses, the nature and properties of the figures placed in the various Houses, and the mixture of other figures affecting them. The Index of the Figure—which geomancers for the most part have used—is found in the following way within this system.

But here we shall give you the secret of the entire Art: how to find the <term>Index|Also known as the "Judge," this final figure summarizes the answer to the divination.</term> in the subsequent Figure. You must count all the points contained in the lines of the projections, and...

and this you shall divide by twelve: and that which remaineth count out <note>original: "project"</note> from the <term>Ascendant|The First House of the chart, representing the beginning of the question or the person asking.</term> through the several Houses; and upon which House there falleth a final remainder <note>original: "unity"</note>, that Figure gives you a proper judgment of the thing <term>sought|original: "quesited"; the specific matter or question being asked about.</term>; and this is used together with the meanings of the judgments mentioned before. But if on either part they shall be equal, or ambiguous, then the **Index** <note>A final figure used to resolve the reading.</note> alone shall clarify for you the thing sought. ->*Additional Symbols: The Moon, The Dragon's Head, and the Sun*<-

It remains now that we declare which matters belong to which House in a question. Then, we will show what every Figure signifies concerning all questions in every House.

First, therefore, we shall handle the meanings of the **Houses**; which are these:

The **first House** shows the person of the <term>Querent|The individual who is asking the question of the oracle.</term>, such as

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues the instructions for geomantic divination. Having previously explained how to determine the "Index" or final figure of a chart on page 9, Agrippa now defines the specific meanings and areas of life governed by each of the twelve houses, which is essential for interpreting the figures placed within them.</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>
<header>Henry Cornelius Agrippa,</header>
<meta>catchword: Fortune</meta>

...whenever a Question shall be proposed concerning himself, his own matters, or anything appertaining to him. And this <term>House|One of the twelve divisions of the geomantic chart, each representing a specific area of human experience.</term> declares the Judgment of the life, form, state, condition, habit, disposition, figure, and the physical appearance <note>original: "colour"</note> of men. The second House contains the Judgment of <term>substance|Material wealth, property, or possessions.</term>, riches, poverty, gain and loss, good fortune and evil fortune: and of accidents regarding substance, such as theft, loss, or negligence. The third House signifies brothers, sisters, and <term>Collaterals in blood|Extended family members who share a common ancestor but are not in a direct line of descent, like cousins.</term>: It judges small journeys and the reliability <note>original: "fidelities"</note> of men. The fourth House signifies fathers and grandfathers, patrimony and inheritance, possessions, buildings, fields, treasure, and hidden things: It also provides a description of those who have lost anything through theft, misplacement, or negligence. The fifth House gives judgment of <term>Legats|This term can refer to both official messengers (legates) or, more likely here, bequests and gifts (legacies).</term>, Messengers, Rumors, and News; of Honor, and of events occurring after death; and of Questions that may be proposed concerning pregnant women or pregnant creatures. The sixth House gives Judgment of infirmities and medicines; of <term>familiars|Close associates or members of a household; can also refer to domestic spirits in an occult context.</term> and servants; of cattle and domestic animals. The seventh House signifies wedlock, <term>whoredom|In this period, a general term for illicit sexual relations or adultery.</term>, and fornication; it renders Judgment of friends, strifes, and controversies; and of matters argued before Judges. The eighth has signification of death, and of those things which come by death, such as bequests <note>original: "Legats"</note> and inheritances; and of the dowry or portion of a wife. The ninth House shows journeys, faith, and constancy; dreams, divine Sciences, and Religion. The tenth House has signification of Honors and of <term>Magisterial Offices|High-ranking public or government positions of authority.</term>. The eleventh House signifies friends and the wealth <note>original: "substance"</note> of Princes. The twelfth House signifies enemies, servants, imprisonment, and misfortune, and whatever evil can happen besides death and sickness, the Judgments of which are to be sought in the sixth House and in the eighth.

It remains now for us to show you what every Figure previously mentioned signifies in these places; which we shall now unfold.

<summary>This page defines the traditional meanings of the twelve houses in geomancy, categorizing every aspect of human life—from wealth and family to illness and death—into specific containers for divination. This framework allows the practitioner to interpret how a specific geomantic figure behaves depending on which house it occupies.</summary>

# of Geomancy.
<page-num>11</page-num>

<margin>The Greater Fortune</margin>
<term>The Greater Fortune|Known in Latin as *Fortuna Major*, this geomantic figure consists of a cluster of dots representing the Sun; it is associated with great success, protection, and inner strength.</term> <note>original: "Fortuna major"</note> being found in the first House, gives a long life and frees one from the suffering of diseases: it shows a man to be noble, high-minded, of good character, of average height, with a healthy <note>original: "ruddy"</note> complexion, curling hair, and his upper body larger than his lower parts. In the second House, it signifies clear wealth and clear gain, good fortune, and the finding of anything lost or mislaid; the capturing of a thief, and the recovery of stolen items. In the third House, it signifies brothers and relatives, nobles, and people of good conduct; it indicates that journeys will be prosperous and profitable with honor: it demonstrates men to be faithful and their friendship to be sincere. In the fourth House, it represents a father who is noble, of good reputation, and known by many people: It expands possessions in cities, increases <term>patrimonies|Inherited estates or wealth passed down from a father or ancestors.</term>, and reveals hidden treasures. In this place, it likewise indicates theft <note>Context suggests this refers to identifying a theft or its resolution</note> and recovers everything lost. In the fifth House, it gives joy through children and causes them to achieve great honors: it makes <term>embassies|Missions or official messages sent by a representative.</term> prosperous, though they are obtained with effort and appeals: It marks rumors as being true: it bestows public honors and causes a man to be very famous after death: it foretells that a woman with child will bring forth a male child. In the sixth House, it frees one from diseases; it shows that those who have illnesses shall recover in a short time; it signifies a physician to be faithful and honest in administering good <term>physic|An archaic term for medicine or medical treatment.</term>, of which there should be no suspicion; it indicates household servants and ministers to be faithful: and of animals, it chiefly signifies horses. In the seventh House, it gives a wife who is rich, honest, and of good character; loving and pleasant: it overcomes strife and arguments. But if the question concerns these disputes, it signifies that the adversaries are very powerful and have great influence. In the eighth House, if a question is asked regarding the death of anyone, it signifies they shall live: it shows the kind of death to be good and natural; an honest burial and honorable funerals: It foretells a wife having a rich <term>dowry|Property or money brought by a bride to her husband on their marriage.</term>, legacies, and inheritance. In the ninth House, it signifies journeys to be pro—

[...pros]perous; and by land on horseback, rather than on foot, indicating a journey that is long and not quickly finished. <note>In geomancy, the "Greater Fortune" is a stable figure, implying that while a journey is successful, it moves with a deliberate, slow pace.</note> He shows the return of those who are absent; signifies men to be of good faith and constant in their intentions; and religious; and those who never change or alter their faith. He foretells that dreams will be true; he signifies true and perfect <term>Sciences|In this historical context, 'sciences' refers to any systematic branch of knowledge, including philosophy, theology, and the occult arts.</term>. In the tenth House, he foretells great honors, bestows public offices, <term>Magistracie|The office or power of a magistrate; high-ranking civil positions of authority.</term>, and judicial roles; and honors in the courts of Princes. He signifies judges to be just and not corrupted by gifts; he brings a legal case to be easily and quickly resolved. He shows Kings to be potent, fortunate, and victorious; he denotes that victory is certain. He signifies a mother to be noble and of long life. In the eleventh house, he signifies true and profitable friends; a Prince who is rich and liberal; and he makes a man fortunate and beloved by his Prince. In the twelfth House, if a question is asked about the quality of enemies, it demonstrates them to be potent and noble, and hardly to be resisted. But if a question concerns any other condition or respect regarding enemies, he will deliver the seeker from their treacheries. It signifies faithful servants; brings back fugitives; has significance over animals such as horses, lions, and bulls; frees one from imprisonments; and he either lessens or takes away imminent dangers.

<margin>*The Lesser Fortune*</margin>
<term>The Lesser Fortune|Known in Latin as *Fortuna Minor*, this geomantic figure is composed of dots representing the Sun, but it is considered less stable and more external than its 'Greater' counterpart.</term> <note>original: "Fortuna minor"</note> in the first house, gives long life, but one burdened with various troubles and illnesses. It signifies a person of short stature and a lean body, having a mole or mark on their forehead or right eye. In the second House, he signifies <term>substance|One's physical wealth, property, or financial standing.</term>, but indicates it will be consumed by too much <term>prodigality|Extravagant wastefulness or reckless spending.</term>. He hides a thief, and a stolen item is unlikely to be recovered except with great labor. In the third House, he causes discord among brothers and relatives; he threatens danger to be present during a journey, though the traveler escapes it. He renders men to be of good faith, but of a secretive and hidden mind. In the fourth House, he causes harm to <term>Patrimonies|Inherited estates or wealth passed down through the family line.</term> and inheritances; he conceals treasures; and lost things cannot be regained except with great difficulty. <margin>The Way</margin>
*Via* <note>Latin: "The Way." This geomantic figure, represented by a single column of four dots, is associated with the Moon and signifies change, movement, and journeys.</note> in the first House, bestoweth a long and prosperous life; giveth signification of a stranger; lean of body, and tall of stature; fair of complexion, having a small beard: a person liberal and pleasant; but slow, and little addicted to labour. In the second, he increaseth substance and riches; recovereth any thing that is stolen or lost; but signifies the thief to be departed without the City. In the third, he multiplies brethren and kinsfolks; signifies continual journeys, and prosperous; men that are publicly known, honest, and of good conversation. *Via* in the fourth House, signifies the father to be honest; increaseth the Patrimony and Inheritance; produceth wealthy fields; showeth treasure to be in the place enquired after; recovereth any thing lost. In the fifth, he increaseth the company of male-children; showeth a woman with child to bring forth a male-child; sendeth Embassages <note>Embassies or official diplomatic missions.</note> to strange and remote parts; increaseth public honours; signifieth an honest kind of death, and to be known through many Provinces. In the sixth House, *Via* preserveth from sickness; signifies the diseased speedily to recover; giveth profitable servants, and animals fruitful and profitable. In the seventh House, he bestoweth a wife fair and pleasant, with whom you shall enjoy perpetual felicity: causeth strifes and controversies most speedily to be determined; adversaries to be easily overcome, and that shall willingly submit their controversies to the Arbitration of good men. ### of Geomancie.

[Via in the ninth house is beneficial] <note>The ninth house traditionally governs long-distance travel, religion, and philosophy.</note> especially by Sea, and portends very great gains to be acquired thereby: he denoteth Priesthoods, and profits from Ecclesiastical <note>Relating to the church or religious leadership.</note> employments; maketh men of good Religion, upright, and constant of faith: sheweth dreams to be true, whose signification shall suddenly appear: increaseth Philosophical and Grammatical Sciences, and those things which appertain to the instruction and bringing up of children. In the tenth House <note>The tenth house governs career, social standing, and authority.</note> if *Via* <note>original: "The Way"</note> be found, he maketh Kings and Princes happie and fortunate, and such as shall maintain continual peace with their Allies; and that they shall require amity <note>Friendship and peaceful relations.</note> and friendship amongst many Princes by their several Embassages <note>Diplomatic missions or embassies.</note>: promoteth publike Honours, Offices, and Magistracie <note>The office or function of a magistrate/judge.</note> amongst the vulgar <note>At the time, "vulgar" simply meant the common people or the general public.</note> and common people; or about things pertaining to the water, journeys, or about gathering Taxes and Assesments: sheweth Judges to be just and merciful, and that shall quickly dispatch Causes <note>Legal cases.</note> depending before them: and denotes a mother to be of good repute, healthy, and of long life. In the eleventh House <note>The house of friendships, alliances, and hopes.</note>, he raiseth many wealthy friends, and acquireth faithful friends in forraign Provinces and Countries, and that shall willingly relieve him that requires them, with all help and diligence: It ingratiates persons with profit and trust amongst Princes, employing him in such Offices, as he shall be incumbered with continual travels. *Via* in the twelfth House <note>The house of hidden enemies, isolation, and imprisonment.</note>, causeth many enemies, but such as of whom little hurt or danger is to be feared: signifies servants and animals to be profitable: whosoever is in prison, to be escaped, or speedily to be delivered from thence: and preserveth a man from the evil accidents of Fortune.

[De]formity <note>Physical abnormality or imperfection.</note> he <note>Referring to the geomantic figure 'Via' (The Way) discussed on the previous page.</note> signifies to proceed from his birth: but if in the fifth House <note>The astrological house traditionally governing children, joy, and legacy.</note>, if he be encompassed with malevolent Aspects <note>Inauspicious or "bad" geometric relationships between points in an astrological chart.</note>, then that monstrousness is to come.

In the second House <note>The house governing personal wealth, earnings, and material possessions.</note>, *The People* <note>original: "Populus"; a geomantic figure associated with the Moon, representing a crowd or stability through sheer numbers.</note> showeth a mean <note>Average or modest.</note> substance, and that to be gotten with great difficulty: it makes a man also always sensible of laborious toil: stolen things are never regained: what is lost shall never be wholly recovered: that which is hidden shall not be found. But if the Question be concerning a thief, it declares him not yet to have fled away, but to lie lurking within the City.

In the third House <note>The house of siblings, neighbors, and short-distance travel.</note>, *The People* raiseth few friends, either of brethren or kindred: it foreshows journeys, but with labour and trouble; notwithstanding some profit may accrue <note>Result or follow as a natural growth.</note> by them: it denotes a man unstable in his faith, and causes a man often to be deceived by his companions.

In the fourth House <note>The house of the home, the father, and ancestral land.</note>, it signifies a father to be sickly, and of a laborious life, and his earthly possessions and inheritances to be taken away: it shows profit to be gained by water: it shows treasure not to be hidden; or if there be any hidden, that it shall not be found: A patrimony <note>An inheritance from one's father or ancestors.</note> to be preserved with great labour.

In the fifth House, he shows no honest Messages, but either makes the messengers to be Porters <note>Low-status laborers who carry burdens.</note>, or public carriers: he divulges false rumors, which notwithstanding have the likeness of some truth, and seem to have their origin from truth, which is not reported as it actually happened: It signifies a woman to be barren, and causes such as are pregnant to have miscarriages: it appoints an inglorious funeral, and ill report after death.

In the sixth House <note>The house of health, illness, and servants.</note>, *The People* showeth cold sicknesses <note>In medieval medicine, illnesses caused by an excess of "cold" humors, such as phlegm.</note>, and chiefly affects the lower parts of the body: A Physician is declared to be careless and negligent in administering medicine to the sick, and signifies those that are affected with sickness to be in danger of death, and scarcely recover at all: it notes the deceitfulness of servants, and detriment <note>Harm or loss.</note> of livestock.

In the seventh House <note>The house of marriage, partnership, and open enemies.</note>, it showeth a wife to be fair and pleasant, but one that shall be pursued by the love of many suitors: it signifies her loves to be feigned and dissembling: it makes weak and impotent adversaries soon to desert their prosecution.

# Henry Cornelius Agrippa

<note>This text continues the interpretation of the fifth house, which concerns children and creative works.</note> signifies many children of both sexes, but more males than females; shows a woman to be with child, and that she shall be delivered without danger: and if a question be propounded concerning any sex, it signifies it to be masculine; increases gainful and profitable embassies and messages, but does not extend fame far after death, yet causes a man to be inherited by his own heirs, and signifies rumors to be true. 

In the sixth house <note>The house of health, service, and small animals.</note> it signifies many and grievous sicknesses, and those likely to continue for a long time; it makes the sick to be in danger of death, and often to die: yet it declares a physician to be learned and honest; gives many servants and cattle, and gains to be acquired from them. 

In the seventh house <note>The house of marriage, partnerships, and open enemies.</note> it signifies a wife to be rich, but either a widow or a woman of a well-grown age; signifies suits and contentions <note>legal disputes</note> to be great and durable, and that love and wedlock shall be effected by lot <note>determined by fate or chance</note>. 

In the eighth house <note>The house of death, inheritance, and the partner's finances.</note>, if a man be inquired after, it shows him to be dead; it signifies the kind of death to be short, and sickness to last but a few days; discovers very profitable legacies and inheritances, and signifies a wife to have a rich dowry. 

In the ninth house <note>The house of long journeys, religion, and higher learning.</note> it signifies long and profitable journeys; shows that if any one be absent, he shall soon return; causes gain to be obtained from religious and ecclesiastical persons or scholars, and signifies a man of a true and perfect science <note>knowledge or expertise</note>. 

In the tenth house <note>The house of career, social standing, and authority.</note>, it makes princes to enlarge their dominions; a judge favorable, but one that must be continually presented with gifts; causes offices and magistracy to be very gainful; signifies a mother who is rich and happy. 

In the eleventh house <note>The house of friendships and hopes.</note>, <term>Gain</term> <note>original: "Acquisitio"</note> multiplies friends, brings profit from them, and increases favor with princes. 

In the twelve house <note>The house of hidden enemies, imprisonment, and large animals.</note> it signifies a man shall have many powerful or potent enemies; reduces <note>leads back</note> and brings home servants who have fled away, and cattle that have strayed; and signifies that he who is in prison shall not be delivered.
