<page-num>86</page-num>
<header>Magical Elements,</header>

*He powerfully commands over you: and by the power of the celestial Spirits who are your lords: and by the person of the exorcist, being conjured, make haste to come and obey your teacher, who is called Octinomos <note>original: "Octinomos" — a Greek-derived name meaning "having eight names," used here as a title of authority</note>. These things being finished, hiss into the four corners of the world. And you shall see immediately great motions: and when you see them, you shall say: Why do you delay? Why do you linger? What are you doing? Prepare yourselves and obey your teacher, in the name of the Lord Bathat, or Vachat rushing over Abrac, coming upon, Abeor over Aberer <note>These are "barbarous names of power"—words from ancient or obscure origins used in magic for their sound and traditional potency rather than literal meaning</note>.*

*Then they will immediately come in their own proper form. And when you see them near the Circle, show them the Pentacle covered with a sacred linen cloth, and let it be uncovered, and say: Behold your conclusion <note>meaning the symbol of their subjection or the seal that binds them</note>, do not be disobedient. And suddenly you will see them in a peaceful form: and they will say to you: Ask what you wish, because we are ready to fulfill all your commands, for the Lord has subjected us to these things. When, however, the Spirits have appeared, then you shall say: Welcome, Spirits, or most noble kings, because I have called you through Him at whom every knee bows, of those in heaven, on earth, and under the earth: in whose hand are all the kingdoms of kings, nor is there anyone who can be contrary to His Majesty. Inasmuch as I constrain you, so that you remain here before the circle visible and affable, for as long and as constant as needed, nor shall you depart without my license, until you truthfully and without any deceit perform my will, by the power of His virtue, who set a boundary for the sea which it cannot pass, and by the law of that power, it does not cross its limits, namely the God most high, king, and lord, who created all things, Amen.* Then command what you will, and it shall be done. Afterwards license them thus:

> † In the name of the Father, † of the Son, and † of the Holy Spirit, go in peace to your places: and let there be peace between us and you, and be ready to come when called.

These are the things which <term>Peter de Abano</term> <note>Petrus de Abano (c. 1250–1316), an Italian physician and philosopher often associated with the *Heptameron*, though his actual authorship is debated by scholars</note> hath spoken concerning Magical Elements.

But that you may the better know the manner of composing a Circle, I will set down one Scheme; so that if any one would make a Circle in Spring-time for the first hour of the Lord's Day <note>Sunday</note>, it must be in the same manner as is the figure following.

Alpha and Omega <note>original: "Al-pha et $\omega$" — the first and last letters of the Greek alphabet, symbolizing that God is the beginning and the end of all things.</note>

## The perfume of the Lord's day <note>Sunday</note>.

Red Wheat.

# The Conjuration of the Lord's day.

I conjure and confirm upon you, strong and holy angels of God, in the name of Adonay, Eye, Eye, Eya <note>These are phonetic variations of the Hebrew name "Ehyeh," meaning "I AM."</note>, who is he who was, is, and shall be, Eye, Abraye; and in the name of Saday, Cados, Cados, Cados <note>Shaddai (Almighty) and Kadosh (Holy).</note>, sitting on high above the Cherubim; and by the great name of God himself, strong and powerful, and exalted above all the heavens, Eye, Saraye, the fashioner of the ages, who created the world, the heaven, the earth, the sea, and all things that are in them on the first day, and sealed them with his holy name Phaa; and by the names of the holy angels who rule in the fourth host, and serve before the most powerful Salamia, a great and honored angel; and by the name of the star which is the Sun, and by its sign, and by the immense name of the living God, and by all the names aforesaid, I conjure you, Michael, great angel, who are set over the Lord's Day; and by the name Adonay, the God of Israel, who created the world and whatever is in it, that you labor for me, and fulfill all my petition, according to my will and my desire, in my business and cause...

<summary>This page completes the list of angels for the four quarters for Sunday and provides the specific conjuration used to invoke the Archangel Michael and the solar spirits.</summary>

<header>Magical Elements,</header>

"...my cause." <note>original: "causa mea"</note> And here you shall declare your cause and business, and for what purpose you make this Conjuration. The Spirits of the Air of the Lord’s day <note>Sunday</note> are under the North Wind; their nature is to procure gold, gems, carbuncles <note>A term used in older texts for any red gemstone, most commonly a garnet</note>, and riches; to help one obtain favor and benevolence; to dissolve the enmities of men; to raise men to honors; and to carry or take away illnesses. But the manner in which they appear has already been described in the former book of Magical Ceremonies <note>A likely reference to the "Fourth Book of Occult Philosophy," a famous grimoire of the period</note>.

> *Holy, Brothers, Brothers, Yah, Yah, Strong One, Yah <note>original: "Cados, Achim, Achim, Ja, Ja, Fortis, Ja"</note>, who appeared on Mount Sinai, with the glorification of the King, the Lord, the Almighty, the Lord of Hosts, Anathay, Yah, Yah, Yah, Our Lord Comes <note>original: "Adonay, Saday, Zebaoth, Anathay, Ya, Ya, Ya, Marinata"</note>, Abim, I Am <note>original: "Jeia," likely a variant of Ehyeh</note>, who created the seas, the ponds, and all waters on the second day <note>A reference to the biblical Creation, where the firmament divided the waters on the second day.</note>, some above the heavens and some on the earth. He sealed the sea by his high name, and the boundary which he set for it shall not be passed: and by the names of the Angels who rule in the first army, who serve the great Angel Orphaniel, who is precious and honored: and by the name of the Star, which is the Moon: and by the names aforesaid, I conjure upon you, namely Gabriel, who are set over the second day, Monday <note>original: "diei Lunae secundo"</note>, that you labor for me and fulfill [my requests], etc.* As in the Conjuration of Sunday.

The Spirits of the Air of Monday are subject to the West wind, which is the wind of the Moon: their nature is to give silver; to convey things from place to place; to make horses swift, and to disclose the secrets of persons both present and future: but in what manner they appear, you may see in the former book <note>This refers to the "First Book" of the Lemegeton or a similar grimoire describing the physical manifestations of spirits.</note>.

# The Conjuration of Tuesday.

I conjure and confirm upon you, O strong and holy Angels, by the names Ya, Ya, Ya; He, He, He; Va, Hy, Hy; Ha, Ha, Ha; Va, Va, Va; An, An, An; Aie, Aie, Aie; El, Ay; Elibra, Elohim, Elohim; <note>original: "Ya, Ya, Ya, He, He, He, Va, Hy, Hy, Ha, Ha, Ha, Va, Va, Va, An, An, An, Aie, Aie, Aie, El, Ay, Elibra, Eloim, Eloim." These are phonetic renderings of Divine names, often derived from Hebrew.</note> and by the names of the high God himself, who made the dry land appear from the water and called it Earth, and brought forth trees and herbs from it, and sealed it with his precious, honored, fearful, and holy name; and by the name of the angels ruling in the fifth army, who serve Acimoy, the great, strong, powerful, and honored Angel; and by the name of the Star, which is Mars; and by the names aforesaid I conjure over you, Samael, the great Angel who is set over the day of Mars; and by the names of Adonai, the living and true God, that you labor for me and fulfill [my petitions], etc. As in the Conjuration of Sunday.

The Spirits of the Air of Tuesday are under the East wind: their nature is to cause wars, mortality, death, and fires; and to provide two thousand Soldiers at a time; to bring death, infirmities, or health. The manner of their appearing you may see in the former book. <note>This refers to the earlier descriptions of "Spirits of the Air" who often appear in terrifying or military forms when associated with Mars.</note>

# Of Peter de Abano.

But this is to be observed in passing, that the first hour of the day, of every Country; and in every season whatsoever, is to be assigned to the **Sun-rising**, when he first appears arising in the horizon: and the first hour of the night is to be the thirteenth hour, from the first hour of the day. <note>This system uses "unequal hours" or "planetary hours." Unlike our modern 60-minute hours, these hours vary in length depending on the season, as the "day" always consists of 12 hours from sunrise to sunset, and the "night" consists of 12 hours from sunset to sunrise.</note> But of these things it is sufficiently spoken.

<image-desc>A horizontal decorative border made of repeated floral fleurons.</image-desc>

->THE END.<- <note>original: "FINIS."</note>

<image-desc>A horizontal decorative border made of repeated floral fleurons.</image-desc>

### ...of the Sun.

But this is also to be observed in passing, that the first hour of the day in every country, and in every region whatsoever, is to be assigned to the **Sun-rising**, at the moment when it first appears emerging on the horizon: and the first hour of the night is to be the thirteenth hour, counting from the first hour of the day. <note>The author is describing "planetary hours." In this system, the time between sunrise and sunset is divided into 12 equal parts, and the time between sunset and sunrise is divided into another 12. Because the length of daylight changes with the seasons, these hours are usually longer or shorter than a standard 60-minute hour.</note> But concerning these things, enough has been spoken.

<image-desc>A horizontal decorative border composed of repeating floral and fleur-de-lis motifs.</image-desc>

->THE END.<- <note>original: "FINIS."</note>

<image-desc>A horizontal decorative border composed of repeating floral and fleur-de-lis motifs.</image-desc>

<term>Sun-rising</term> <note>The start of the magical day. Unlike the civil day which begins at midnight, the magical day begins at the exact moment of dawn.</note>
<term>thirteenth hour</term> <note>The first hour of the night. In the 24-hour cycle of planetary hours, the twelve hours of the day are followed by the twelve hours of the night.</note>

<header>Of the Nature of Spirits.</header>

# <term>ISAGOGE</term>: <note>From the Greek *eisagoge*, meaning an "introduction" or "preparatory handbook" to a specific subject.</note>
## An Introductory Discourse on the na-
## ture of such Spirits as are active within the
## <term>sublunary</term> Bounds; <note>Sublunary: Literally "under the moon." In the traditional worldview, this refers to the Earth and its atmosphere—the realm of change, decay, and elemental forces—as opposed to the perfect, unchanging heavens above the moon.</note> their Origin, Names, Of-
## fices, Illusions, Power, Prophesies, Miracles; and how
## they may be expelled and driven away.

### By George Pictorius of Villingen, Doctor of <term>Medicine</term>. <note>original: "Physick." In the 16th century, medicine (physick) was deeply intertwined with philosophy and the study of the natural and supernatural worlds.</note>

->In a Discourse between<-
## CASTOR and POLLUX.

---

<margin>Castor.</margin>
<image-desc>A decorative square woodcut initial 'T' containing a figure seated at a desk or chair amidst scrolling patterns.</image-desc>The Greeks report that Castor and Pol-
lux both emerged from a single egg; 
but I find this hard to believe, given 
the great difference in your minds. For 
you, Pollux, strive for the heavens, but 
the other <note>The text says "she," likely a personification of the mortal nature or a transcription error for "the other" (the mortal Castor), who is focused on earthly, violent matters.</note> dwells upon the earth and its 
slaughters.

<margin>Pollux.</margin>
And perhaps that is where the 
argument comes from, that the Greeks 
were always granted the freedom to lie.

<margin>Castor.</margin>
Most certainly.

<meta>catchword: Pollux.</meta>

<summary>This page serves as the title and opening dialogue for George Pictorius’s "Isagoge," a treatise on the nature of spirits inhabiting the earthly realm. The text begins with a classical dialogue between the mythological twins Castor and Pollux to frame the philosophical inquiry.</summary>
<keywords>Isagoge, spirits, sublunary realm, Georg Pictorius, Castor and Pollux, demonology, Renaissance medicine</keywords>

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the twins Castor and Pollux, who serve as the internal narrators for Pictorius’s treatise. While the previous page established their differing temperaments, this page shifts into a philosophical debate over the dual nature of humanity and the definition of "spirits" or "daemons."</meta>

<page-num>110</page-num>
<header>A Discourse,</header>

Pollux. But it is not to be supposed that the Greeks are boastful in all things; but rather like many others when they speak from a three-footed tripod <note>original: "three-footed thing." This refers to the Delphic Tripod upon which the Pythian priestess sat to deliver oracles.</note>; of which also the Poet *Ovid* speaks in verse,

->--- The Greeks do not invent everything. <note>original Latin: "Nec fingunt omnia Graeci."</note><-

<margin>Man is a God to man. <note>original Latin: "Homo homini Deus."</note></margin>
Castor. In this proverb I protest they are most true, without any exception: that is, *A man is a spirit to man.* <note>original Greek: "ἄνθρωπος ἀνθρώπῳ δαιμόνιον" (anthrōpos anthrōpō daimonion). While the Greek "daimonion" refers to a divine power or spirit, the text here translates it more harshly as "devil."</note> That is, *One man to another is a devil.*

Pollux. Why do you believe this to be most true, *Castor*?

<margin>Man is a devil to man. <note>original Latin: "Homo homini diabolus."</note></margin>
Castor. Truly, that man to man is a devil and a ravening wolf, daily events do most certainly prove, if we do but note the treacheries that one man invents daily against another: the robberies, thefts, plunderings, rapes, slaughters, deceits, adulteries, and an hundred vipers of this nature; the fathers persecute the son with a serpentine and poisonous biting; one friend seeks to devour another, neither can the guest be safe with his host.

<margin>Pliny, Book 2, Chapter 7.</margin>
Pollux. I confess it is truth you speak; but from what I hear, you do misunderstand the <term>Etymology</term> <note>The study of the origins and historical meanings of words.</note> of the word compared in this Proverb; for *Daemon* <note>The translator uses "Daemon" here in the classical sense of a guardian spirit or a being between gods and men, rather than a purely evil entity.</note> here is not a horrible or odious name, but the name of one that does administer help or succor unto another, and whom *Pliny* calls a God.

Castor. Therefore do you affirm the word *Daemon* in this Proverb to signify anything other than a cunning and malicious accuser? <note>Castor is using the literal meaning of the Greek "diabolos" (accuser/slanderer), the root of "devil."</note>

Pollux. You have not shot wide of the mark: for that there are more *Daemons* than that <term>sublunary</term> <note>Inhabiting the realm below the moon, i.e., the Earthly atmosphere.</note> one which you understand, every one may easily perceive who has not negligently read the opinions of the most excellent *Plato*.

Castor. I desire therefore that you would not conceal such writings; but that I may grasp the very marrow of them.

Pollux. I will embrace your desire, for truly I do delight to discuss this subject with you; mark therefore, and give attention.

<meta>catchword: Plato</meta>

<summary>Pollux and Castor debate the proverb "Man is a spirit/devil to man," leading to a distinction between the malicious "accuser" and the classical "Daemon" as a helpful spirit. Pollux begins to introduce Platonic philosophy to explain the variety of spirits existing in the world.</summary>
<keywords>daemons, etymology, human nature, Pliny, Plato, Ovid, Greek philosophy, spirits, morality</keywords>

--- Page 137 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the twins Castor and Pollux. Following their debate on the "devilish" nature of human behavior, Pollux now introduces a more structured, Neoplatonic classification of spirits to provide a philosophical framework for their discussion.</meta>

### Of the Nature of Spirits.

<margin>Three degrees of Spirits.</margin>
<margin>The first degree of Spirits.</margin>
**Plato** divided the orders of Devils <note>In this context, "Devils" refers to the Greek *daimones*, which can mean spirits, lesser gods, or guiding entities, rather than strictly evil demons.</note> or Spirits into three degrees, which as they are distinct in the greatness of their dignity, so also they are different in the distance and holding of their places. And the first order he ascribes to those Spirits whose bodies are nourished of the most pure element of Air, wrought and joined together, in a manner, as it were with splendid threads, not having so much reference to the element of fire, that they may be visible to the sight; neither do they so much participate of the earth, that they may be touched or felt; and they do inhabit the Celestial Theater, attending and waiting on their Prince, not to be declared by any human tongue, or beyond the commands of the most wise God.

<margin>The second degree.</margin>
But the other degree is derived from those Spirits which **Apuleius** terms <term>rational animals</term> <note>original Latin: "animalia ratione, animo passiva, tempore aeterna." This was a standard medieval definition of spirits: they have reason like humans, but are immortal like gods.</note>, passive in their mind, and eternal in their time, understanding the apostate Spirits spread abroad from the bounds and borders of the Moon, unto us under the dominion of their Prince **Beelzebub**, which before the fall of **Lucifer** had pure clarified bodies; and now, like unto the former, do wander up and down, after their transgression, in the form of an airy quality.

*Castor.* These I do not conceive are understood in the Greek Proverb: for these do hurt, and are the accusers and betrayers of men. But proceed.

<margin>The third degree.</margin>
<margin>*Plato* called *dæm on*, &c.</margin>
*Pollux.* The third degree of Spirits is of a divine deity, which is called by **Hermes**, A divine miracle to man, if he do not degenerate from the Kingly habit of his first form; whom therefore of this kind the Greeks and **Plato** have called **Dæmons** <note>original Greek: *daimōn*. Pollux is explaining that the word originally meant "knowing" or "divine," applying it to wise humans.</note>, that is, God; and that man may be like unto God, and profitable and commodious one to another; and so also (the Syrian being witness) we have known **Plato** himself to have been called **Dæmon**, because he had set forth very many things of very high matters, for the good of the Commonwealth; and so likewise **Aristotle**, because he very largely disputed of <term>sublunaries</term> <note>Things belonging to the Earth and the atmosphere below the moon, which were believed to be subject to change and decay, unlike the eternal heavens.</note>, and all such things as are subject to motion and sense. **Homer** calls God and evil Spirits, *Demons*, without making a distinction.

<meta>catchword: Castor.</meta>

<summary>Pollux explains the three types of spirits according to Platonic tradition: celestial attendants of God, fallen airy spirits under Beelzebub, and "human daemons"—great philosophers like Plato and Aristotle who elevated humanity through wisdom.</summary>

<keywords>Plato, Apuleius, Beelzebub, Hermes Trismegistus, Aristotle, Homer, daemon, hierarchy of spirits, sublunaries, fallen angels</keywords>

--- Page 138 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Castor and Pollux. Having previously discussed the first order of spirits, the speakers now move to the second degree—the "Lunaries"—and explore the nature of demonic knowledge and the physical transformation of fallen angels.</meta>

**Castor.** You have committed the ship to the waves, **Pollux**; therefore, do not cease your progress. Tell us more concerning the office and employment of these spirits, to whom **Plato** attributes the second degree and calls "Lunaries" <note>Spirits associated with the Moon or the sublunary sphere (the region between Earth and the Moon).</note>.

**Pollux.** What shall I say?

**Castor.** First, explain why you previously called these spirits "cunning and much-knowing accusers."

<margin>
Why the devil is said to have much knowledge.
</margin>
**Pollux.** Saint **Augustine** clarifies this difficulty. He says that a "Devil" <note>original: "Diabolus," which Augustine etymologically links to knowledge and accusation.</note> signifies a cunning and highly knowledgeable sharpness of mind. Through his understanding of how the "seeds" of the elements mix together, he knows the secrets and hidden powers of the world. He takes things that would normally happen slowly and naturally over time and, by speeding up those natural processes or using his own skill, he brings them to pass much sooner. He gives an example of this in the wise men of **Pharaoh**, who immediately produced frogs and serpents at the King’s command—things that nature produces much more slowly.

**Castor.** You have answered the question excellently, **Pollux**; but add something concerning the origin of those spirits who resist and refuse virtue. For I have often doubted and been persuaded that such **Furies** <note>original: "Erynnes," the Greek deities of vengeance (Furies) often interpreted in a Christian context as punishing demons.</note> as come from God do not appear from within the earth.

<margin>
Book 2, Distinction 7.
</margin>
**Pollux.** Holy Scripture mentions their origin everywhere, but I will clarify your doubt. Many various opinions exist among writers, but most commonly **Peter Lombard** <note>A 12th-century theologian whose "Book of Sentences" was the standard textbook of medieval theology.</note>, in his *Book of Sentences*, draws his arguments from Saint **Augustine’s** commentary on **Genesis**. He states that before his fall, the Devil was an **Archangel** and had a fine, delicate body created by God out of the clearest and purest matter of the sky and air. However, after his fall from being an **Archangel**, he became an **Apostate** <note>One who has abandoned their faith or divine standing.</note>, and his body was no longer fine and subtle. Instead, his body was changed so that it could suffer the effects of a more "gross" or heavy substance, taking on the quality of a more obscure, dark, and...

<summary>Castor and Pollux discuss why demons possess superior knowledge of natural laws and how the Devil’s body was transformed from pure light/air into a heavier, "gross" substance capable of suffering after his fall.</summary>
<keywords>Lunaries, Saint Augustine, demonic knowledge, Pharaoh's magicians, Furies, Peter Lombard, Archangel, Apostate, spiritual bodies, the Fall</keywords>

--- Page 139 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Castor and Pollux regarding the nature and fall of spirits. Having discussed the cleverness of demons on the previous page, they now turn to the origins of their fall and their current dwelling place according to scripture and Church Fathers.</meta>

# Of the Nature of Spirits.

...thick Air <note>original: "spissious," meaning dense or viscous; early modern science often associated the bodies of spirits with the density of the air they inhabited.</note>, which body also was stricken and astonished with the raging madness of pride, did draw away very many which were then Angels with him into his service and bondage, that they might be made Devils, who for him in this troublesome world do exercise their servile courses for him, and they do compel the inhabitants therein, or rather entice them; and to this purpose they undertake various endeavors, and do attempt various and manifold horrible studies, that are abominable unto God, and they serve in slavery and thralldom to **Beelzebub** their Prince, and are held in most strong captivity.

**Castor.** What? Have we the fall of this Archangel nowhere else in holy writ, but in the writings of **St. Augustine**?

<margin>The fall of Lucifer in Scripture.</margin>
**Pollux.** We have also the fall of other Angels.

**Castor.** Where?

**Pollux.** In **Esaias** <note>The Prophet Isaiah.</note>, to whom thou shalt give the honor of an Evangelist, rather than a Prophet, because he so fully and plainly foretold of Christ and his kingdom: he maketh mention hereof in his 14th Chapter. And we have them also spoken of by the Apostle **Peter**, when he saith, *God spared not his Angels which sinned.* 2 Peter 2:4.

**Castor.** Have the Devils a select place appointed them by God, which they inhabit?

<margin>What place the devils have appointed.</margin>
**Pollux.** **Peter**, the head of the Church, in the place before quoted, affirmeth them to be cast headlong into hell, reserved in the chains of hell, from whence (as **Cornelius** <note>Likely referring to the theologian Cornelius a Lapide, known for his biblical commentaries.</note> saith) they never go out, unless it be to tempt, provoke and delude men. But **St. Augustine**, the Champion of Christ, in his book *The Agony of a Christian* <note>original: "De Agone Christiano."</note>, teacheth, That these kind of Spirits do inhabit in the sublunary region <note>The realm between the Earth and the Moon, traditionally considered the lowest and most corruptible part of the heavens.</note>. And in his 49th Epistle he sets forth, That the most dark and obscure part of the Air, is predestinated unto them as a prison, that they may the more nearly cast their nets of enticing and detaining.

**Castor.** **Origen** <note>An influential early Christian scholar whose views on the temporary nature of hell were later declared heretical.</note> hath taught, That the punishments of the Devils are appointed for a time; what sayest thou to this?

<margin>The torments of the devils are ever-lasting.</margin>
**Pollux.** What shall I say? unless I should bewail and deplore the opinion of so great a man.

**Castor.** Wherefore shouldst thou do so?

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Castor and Pollux. Having discussed the "accuser of the brethren" and "evil angels," Pollux now provides a specific historical example of a haunting in Athens to illustrate the physical manifestations and nature of such spirits.</meta>

# Of the Nature of Spirits.

There was a spacious house at Athens which no one would inhabit because of the nightly hauntings of spirits. These were so terrifying to the residents that even during the day, while they were wide awake, the spirits would cast them into a dreamlike state; the shapes and forms they saw remained vividly in their memories. Eventually, a certain philosopher named *Athenodorus* <note>Athenodorus Cananites (c. 74 BC–7 AD), a Stoic philosopher and teacher of the Emperor Augustus.</note> happened to purchase that house and prepared it to be his home. Because everyone had a bad suspicion regarding the house, he immediately commanded his servants to provide him with a bed and tables so that he might go to bed after finishing his studies. 

He therefore (according to *Pliny* <note>Pliny the Younger (61–c. 113 AD), a Roman author who recorded this famous ghost story in his Letters (Book 7, Letter 27).</note>), when he went in during the evening and applied himself to his studies, suddenly heard the locks shake open and the sound of moving chains. Nevertheless, he did not look up or stir from his book, but instead stopped his ears with his fingers, lest that frantic noise create a groundless fear in him. But as the noise kept coming closer, he finally looked up and saw a specter like a beckoning and calling finger. He paid little attention to it until it had touched him three times and the noise moved near the table. Then he looked up, took a light, and beheld the spirit. It looked like an old man, wasted away with withered thinness and deformity, with a long, hanging beard and horrible, matted hair; his legs and feet were weighed down with chains and fetters. The spirit went toward a bolted gate and there left the philosopher, vanishing away.

**Castor.** What fearful things you tell, **Pollux**! But what was the outcome of this somber sight?

**Pollux.** The next day, he reported the entire matter to the magistrates in the order he had seen it, advising them that they should dig carefully around the threshold of the door. He believed they might find something there that could allow the house to be quiet and habitable.

**Castor.** And what did they find?

**Pollux.** Having dug up the earth, *Pliny* says they found a dead carcass, bound and entangled in chains and fetters, with the flesh consumed by the passage of time. Without delay, they had the remains buried according to Christian ceremonies <note>The original story by the Roman author Pliny describes a proper public burial; the mention of "Christian ceremonies" here is an adaptation by the early modern author to fit their contemporary religious context.</note>.

<meta>catchword: Castor.</meta>

<summary>This page recounts Pliny the Younger's famous ghost story of the philosopher Athenodorus, who investigated a haunted house in Athens. The account describes how the discovery and proper burial of a chained skeleton brought peace to the restless spirit and the home.</summary>
<keywords>haunted house, Athens, Athenodorus, Pliny the Younger, ghosts, chains, exorcism, burial rites, Castor, Pollux</keywords>

*Castor.* But this being performed, did the house afterwards become quiet and habitable?

*Pol.* Yes, very well.

<margin>They are possessed with madness who destroy churchyards. The Spirits Zazelus and Eurynomus.</margin>
*Castor.* What madness therefore possesses those who profane and destroy churchyards, where the sacred organs <note>The "sacred organs" refers to the bodies and remains of the faithful, viewed as instruments of the Holy Spirit.</note> of the holy and blessed Spirit rest; and who give the bones of the dead as meat to the spirit *Zazelus* <note>Zazelus is a variant of Azazel, a spirit often associated with the wilderness or the "scapegoat" in Hebrew tradition. The text refers to the 3rd Book of Kings (known today as 1 Kings), likely referencing the "lying spirit" in chapter 22.</note>, of whom mention is made in the 3rd Book of *Kings*; and we read in *Pausanias* <note>Pausanias (c. 110–180 AD) was a Greek traveler and geographer.</note>, among the histories of *Delphi*, that he was called *Eurynomus* <note>Eurynomus was a demon in Greek mythology who lived in the underworld and stripped the flesh from the corpses of the dead.</note>.

*Pol.* You shall find that the governors of cities who shared the opinion and judgment of Christians did subvert, destroy, and profane these holy places, so that there the youth might dance their mocking interludes to the frantic sound of the drum or tabor, and sing, *Hail, Paean!* <note>original: "Io pæan" — A Greek hymn of triumph or praise, typically associated with Apollo.</note>; or, there the poor, common old women sold cheap trinkets or lupine seeds <note>Lupine seeds were a common, inexpensive food item, sometimes used as play-money in theater.</note>, which God intended to be cleansed with holy prayers for the salvation of souls, or the breaking of bread for the hungry.

*Castor.* But it is a wicked and heathenish thing to have so touched the anointed of God.

<margin>The ceremony of burials was held in great esteem among the Heathens.</margin>
*Pol.* And worse than heathenish; for the heathens highly esteemed the rites and ceremonies of burials, as *Elpenor* <note>Elpenor was a companion of Odysseus in Homer's Odyssey who died and, as a ghost, pleaded for a proper burial.</note> witnesses in *Homer* where he yields up his life; and in *Homer* he speaks to *Ulysses*: *I entreat you, O Ulysses, to be mindful of me, and not to depart from here and leave me unburied, lest, not being rightly buried, I should become the cause of the gods' wrath.* And *Archytas* the philosopher, in *Flaccus* <note>Horace (Quintus Horatius Flaccus), the Roman lyric poet.</note>, speaks thus to the sailor:

<margin>Horace, Book 1 of his poems.</margin>
> The South Wind, that swift companion of the setting Orion, 
> overwhelmed me too in the Illyrian waves. 
> But you, O sailor, do not be so stingy 
> as to withhold a small handful of shifting sand 
> from my unburied bones and head. 
> If you do this, however much the East Wind 
> may threaten the Western waves, 
> let the Venusian woods be lashed while you remain safe, 
> and may a great reward flow down to you 
> from every possible source, through the favor of the gods.
<note>original: "Me quoque divexi Rapidus comes Orionis / Illyricis Notus obruit undis. / At tu Nauta vaga ne parce malignus harenæ, / Ossibus & capiti inhumato. / Particulam dare; sic quocunque minabitur Eurus / Fluctibus Hesperiis, Venusinæ / Plectantur silvæ, te sospite multaque merces / Unde potest tibi defluat æquo." — From Horace, Odes 1.28.</note>

<margin>Aeneid, Book 4.</margin>

<margin>The History of Caius Caligula.</margin>
*Suetonius* <note>Gaius Suetonius Tranquillus (c. 69–122 AD), a Roman historian who wrote "The Twelve Caesars."</note>, as we have shown before, adds the like concerning the dead body of the Emperor *Caius Caligula* in the Gardens of *Lamianus* <note>The Horti Lamiani were a set of private gardens on the Esquiline Hill in Rome where Caligula was hastily buried after his assassination.</note>, being not duly buried; for this body, because it was only covered with a light turf, did very much disquiet and trouble the possessors of the Garden with violent incursions in the night, until it was taken up again by his sisters (who had returned from banishment) and was by them buried rightly and duly.

<margin>The house of Caligula was burned because of the spirits.</margin>
***Castor.*** And the house where that same Emperor died could not be freed from the fury of those shadows or spirits by any other way or means—as history mentions—except by burning it to the ground. <note>The Roman Emperor Caligula was assassinated in his palace in 41 AD; the historian Suetonius records that the site remained haunted until the building was destroyed by fire.</note>

<margin>The mountain of Hechelberg.</margin>
***Pollux.*** **Aristotle**, speaking of miracles, mentions a certain mountain in **Norway** named **Hechelberg**, <note>original: "Hechelberg"; this likely refers to Mount Hekla, which, though located in Iceland, was often associated with Norway in medieval geography and was widely considered a gateway to Hell.</note> surrounded by the sea, which continually sent out such lamentable voices, like the yelling and howling of infernal devils. This was to such an extent that the noise and clamor of their terrible roaring could be heard almost a mile away; and the flocking together of great ravens and vultures near it prevented anyone from approaching.

<margin>A hill in Lyppora.</margin>
And he reports that in **Lyppora**, <note>original: "Lyppora"; modern Lipari, one of the volcanic Aeolian Islands near Sicily.</note> near the **Aeolian Islands**, there was a certain hill where, during the night, people heard cymbals and the sounds of tinkling brass instruments, along with certain secret and hidden screeching, laughing, and the roaring of spirits. But just now, ***Castor***, you made mention of **Zazelus**, <note>A spirit often identified in occult texts with the planet Saturn or desert-dwelling demons.</note> whom you also asserted was called **Eurynomus** <note>In Greek mythology, Eurynomus was a daemon of the underworld who stripped the flesh from the corpses of the dead.</note> by **Pausanias**; <note>A 2nd-century AD Greek traveler and geographer whose descriptions of Greece are a primary historical source.</note> I desire you to tell me something more at length concerning this spirit.

<margin>Zazelus lives on the flesh of the dead.</margin>
***Castor.*** They declare that he lives entirely upon the flesh of the dead, so much so that sometimes he does not even leave the bones.

***Pollux.*** The **Saxon Grammarians**, <note>Referring to Saxo Grammaticus (c. 1160–1220), a Danish historian and author of the *Gesta Danorum* (Deeds of the Danes).</note> in the fifth book of the *Danish History*, very truly give their consent and agreement to this assertion of yours; for there they set before our eyes a remarkable history of a certain **Asuitus** and **Asmundus**, which easily proves all your sayings.

***Castor.*** I beseech you, relate this to me, ***Pollux***.

<margin>A wonderful history of Asuitus and Asmundus.</margin>
***Pollux.*** Pay attention; it is this: **Asuitus** and **Asmundus** had sworn mutual vows to each other that whoever lived the longest would entomb himself alive with the deceased. Now, sickness consumed **Asuitus** before **Asmundus**; whereupon **Asmundus**, for the sake of his oath of friendship, entombed himself alive in a vast, deep den with his dog and his horse. He carried some meat with him, which he fed upon for a long time. At length, **Eric**, the King of **Sweden**, <note>original: "Suecia"</note> came to that place with an army and broke open the tomb of **Asuitus**; (sup-

<margin>
Spirits cannot be distinguished by gender.

<margin>
Not all spirits are capable of taking on multiple shapes.

That spirits sometimes sleep or rest, move from place to place, and reveal themselves visibly to human senses. **Socrates** asserts that a spirit spoke with him—one he occasionally saw and felt—but their bodies cannot be distinguished by gender. However, **Marcus Cherronesus** <note>Likely referring to Michael Psellus, an 11th-century Byzantine scholar whose work *On the Operation of Demons* was a primary source for Renaissance demonologists.</note>, an excellent researcher into the nature of spirits, writes that they possess <term>simple bodies</term> <note>Bodies composed of a single element, such as pure air or fire, rather than a mix of elements like human flesh.</note> and that the distinction of sex belongs only to "compound" bodies. Yet, their bodies are easily moved and flexible, naturally suited to take on any shape. *For, he says, just as the clouds appear to take the form of men or whatever else you imagine, so too do the bodies of spirits take on various shapes as they please; because of this, they can transform into the likeness of men or women.* 

Nevertheless, this ability is not available to all of them, but only to spirits of fire and air. For he teaches that water spirits have slower and less active bodies; because of the slow and soft nature of that element, they most often resemble birds or women. The **Naiads** and **Nereids** <note>Water nymphs from Greek mythology, inhabiting springs and the sea respectively.</note> celebrated by the poets are of this kind. **Trithemius** <note>original: "Trimetius"; Johannes Trithemius (1462–1516) was a famous German abbot and occult scholar.</note> testifies that demons prefer to assume human shapes over any other form; however, they do so only when they cannot find air that is suitable for that purpose. He says that they create these appearances from whatever opposing <term>humors</term> <note>In ancient medicine, "humors" were bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, etc.) thought to dictate health and temperament; here used to mean physical vapors.</note> or vapors are available. Thus, they are sometimes seen in the form and shape of a lion, a wolf, a sow, a donkey, a centaur, or a horned man with goat-like feet. Such creatures were reportedly seen in the mountains of **Thuringia** <note>A region in central Germany famous for its folklore and the Kyffhäuser mountain.</note>, where a terrible roaring was heard.

**Castor.** **Porphyry** (as cited by **Eusebius** in the fourth book of his *Preparation for the Gospel* <note>original: "Evangelical Preparation" or *Praeparatio Evangelica*.</note>) teaches that some of these are good spirits and some are bad; but I have always considered them all to be evil.

**Pollux.** Then it seems you are not swayed by the claims of **Porphyry**, **Apuleius**, **Proclus**, or other Platonists mentioned in **St. Augustine’s** book *The City of God* <note>original: "De Civitate Dei".</note> (Books 1, 2, and 3), who

Neither in the Gospel could the Devils hurt the Swine until **Christ** had given them leave.

# Of the Nature of Spirits.

...through his counsel he lets loose his chains, so that he might then defend and mercifully preserve us.

*Pollux.* You say well; for just as a wild boar is not to be feared if he is bound and held with a strong chain by a powerful man who is able by his strength to restrain the fierceness of the boar; but rather the man is to be feared and requested that he would not let loose the boar. So also **Satan** is not to be feared, being bound with the cords of the **Almighty**; but the **Almighty** rather, who holds him with a cord, lest at any time He should let loose his cord to execute his will against us.

*Pollux.* If it pleases God, they have very much power; but take a demonstration of this, *Castor*, from the **Chaldeans**, among whom the Devil raged with so much power and dominion that they made no account of the true God, but instead worshipped the elements. There is no need for a demonstration regarding the **Greeks**; for the fury of the Devil reigned so much among them that, by his arguments, they considered **Saturn** to be a very great God—even though he devoured his own children <note>A reference to the myth of Cronus/Saturn, who ate his offspring to prevent them from overthrowing him.</note>. There is no man who is ignorant that the **Egyptians** have been worse than the **Greeks**, when they made peculiar Gods for themselves through the soulless persuasions of the Devil; for one worshipped a sheep, another a goat, another a calf. Very many worshipped hogs, crows, hawks, vultures, eagles, crocodiles, cats, dogs, wolves, asses, and dragons—and even growing things, such as onions, garlic, and thorns. Every person who is eager for reading shall find this in **John of Damascus** <note>original: "Damascenu"; a 7th-8th century monk and theologian.</note> in his *History of Josaphat and Barlaam* <note>A popular medieval Christian story loosely based on the life of the Buddha.</note>, and in **Eusebius** <note>Eusebius of Caesarea, a 4th-century historian of the early Christian Church.</note>, in the fourth <meta>book</meta>

book, and first Chapter of the *Preparations for the Gospel* <note>original: *Evangelical Preparations*; a reference to the work *Praeparatio Evangelica* by the early Christian scholar Eusebius of Caesarea.</note>; neither do I consider the **Hebrews** (who boast of being the descendants of their father **Abraham**) to have been any better than the others. <meta>This page continues the dialogue between Castor and Pollux regarding the deceptive nature of demons. -> # Of the Nature of Spirits. <-

...a stronger and more prevalent power of fore-knowledge than men of earthly bodies can have; or because of the incomparable swiftness of their **airy bodies** <note>Early Christian and medieval theologians often believed that demons were not purely spiritual but possessed "airy" or "ethereal" bodies that allowed them to move faster than any physical creature.</note>, which wonderfully exceeds not only the speed of men and wild beasts, but also the flight of birds. By this means, they are able to declare things long before they come to be known by us—things which we, by reason of the earthly slowness of our senses, never cease to wonder at and admire. Or, perhaps it is because of the benefit of their continual life that they obtain this wonderful experience of things; an experience which we cannot attain because of the shortness of our momentary life, which is like a bubble.

*Pollux.* This last assertion of ***Saint Augustine*** <note>Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), one of the most influential Church Fathers, who wrote extensively on the nature of demons in works like *The City of God*.</note> seems to me to be more true than the rest, because the passing of many years brings great experience.

*Castor.* If any one shall deny these opinions of *Augustine* as being in error, ***Damascenus*** <note>original: *Damascenus*; referring to St. John of Damascus (c. 675–749 AD), a prominent monk and theologian.</note> sets a greater witness of these things, without any exception, before our eyes; who in his second book of *The Orthodox Faith* <note>original: *De Fide Orthodoxa*</note> says the following:

<margin>The devils of themselves cannot foreknow things to come</margin>

> That the devils cannot foreknow things to come, for that belongs only unto God; but so much as they are able to know, they have from the arrangement of the <term>celestial and inferior bodies</term> <note>This refers to the "Great Chain of Being" and astrology; the "celestial" bodies are the stars and planets, while "inferior" bodies are those on Earth. Demons were thought to be expert observers of natural patterns.</note>.

*Pollux.* Why therefore do the devils so willingly and of their own accord undertake prophecies, and offer to answer oracles? What benefit do they receive from this?

<margin>Why the devils desire to be counted Prophets.</margin>

*Castor.* Nothing, except that by this means they seek to gain a great reputation, and they covet being counted worthy of admiration, desiring to be adored in the place of Gods.

*Pollux.* We know that the devil is the father of lies, *Castor*. From this, we must piously believe that those things which he foretells, he merely extracts from his own lies.

<margin>Isaiah 41.</margin>

*Castor.* Furthermore, the Prophet ***Isaiah*** <note>original: *Efaias*</note> says thus:

> Show the things that are to come hereafter, and tell us, that we may know that ye are gods...

<margin>2 Peter 1.</margin>

And the Apostle **Peter** also says:

> The prophecy came not in old time by the will of man, but holy men of God spoke as they were moved by the Holy Ghost.

*Pollux.* No man, therefore, will deny that they do sometimes foretell things to come.

*Castor.* No man, certainly; but for what cause that is attained—

<margin>Why the devil sometimes tells the truth.</margin>
...[liste]ned to, Saint John Chrysostom <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407 AD) was an influential Archbishop of Constantinople known for his eloquent preaching; his name literally means "golden-mouthed."</note> teaches most clearly in these words: *It is granted,* he says, *that sometimes the devil does speak the truth, so that he might make his own lies more believable by mixing them with a rare bit of truth. For if he never told the truth, he could deceive no one, nor would his lying be enough for him to tempt people with.* This is what Chrysostom says. Nevertheless, if the devil understands that he does not have the grace granted to him to foretell the truth on his own, he predicts things anyway, but so unclearly—as Saint Augustine <note>Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a foundational Christian theologian who wrote extensively on the nature of evil and the deceptive power of demons in works like *The City of God*.</note> says—<margin>The Oracles of the devils are uncertain.</margin> that he always places the blame for the things he foretold upon the person who interpreted them. Porphyry <note>Porphyry of Tyre (c. 234–305 AD) was a Neoplatonic philosopher and an opponent of early Christianity, though he was often cited by Christian writers as an authority on "pagan" spiritual practices.</note>, in his book of Oracles, although he is the greatest supporter of devils and the most expert teacher of diabolical arts, nevertheless agrees with the aforementioned Doctors of the Church. He says that the foreknowledge of things to come is not only confusing to men, but also uncertain to the gods, and full of many obscurities.

*Pollux.* You have said that the predictions of the devils are done in this manner so they may gain authority for themselves among gullible people and be worshipped instead of Gods. For what purpose then do the evil spirits perform miracles?

*Castor.* What is a miracle, *Pollux*?

<margin>What a miracle is. The devils work miracles.</margin>
*Pollux.* A new and unusual occurrence which happens contrary to the normal course of nature and custom, and which draws men into a state of wonder.

*Castor.* But do they truly perform miracles?

*Pollux.* They do. For why else do you believe that Aesculapius <note>The ancient Greek god of medicine and healing.</note> was honored as a god at his consecration, if not by means of a miracle, when he transported a serpent from Epidaurus to Rome? What gave such great authority to Juno <note>The queen of the Roman gods.</note>, except for the working of a miracle? This happened when her wooden image was asked by Furius Camillus <note>Marcus Furius Camillus (c. 446–365 BC), a Roman soldier and statesman who, according to legend, moved the statue of Juno from the defeated city of Veii to Rome.</note> whether it wanted to be carried to Rome, and it answered with a human voice, "*It would.*" Also, from that point on, Fortune was made a goddess, because her statue <note>original: *Statua*</note>, on the way to Latium, spoke with a human voice not just once, but many times in the hearing of many people. In the 8th Chapter of the *Acts* of the Apostles, we read of miracles done by Simon the son of Rachel <note>Usually identified as Simon Magus, a practitioner of magic who attempted to buy the power of the Holy Spirit from the Apostles.</note>; and in *Exodus* 8, we read of the magici-

<margin>Some miracles occur naturally.</margin>

And when it is caught by this means, it may not be gathered or plucked up without danger; for immediate death follows him that gathers or plucks it up, unless he is protected with a preservative charm <note>original: "Preservative"</note> about his neck, made of the same root. For this reason, those who want the root make incisions <note>original: "scarifie"</note> all around it; and having bound the root with a cord, they tie the same to a dog, and suddenly depart. Whereupon the dog, trying too hard to follow after them, pulls up the root; and, as if the dog were to take the place of his master, he dies immediately; and afterwards the same root may be taken and handled without any danger to any man. <header>Of the Nature of Spirits.</header>

[-ent] <note>This completes a word from the previous page, likely "enticement" or "incitement."</note>: for there are some who suppose that these ghosts are devils, because of the great fear and terror with which they violently harass men at night in their houses; and sometimes, due to their inherent nature, they do actual harm. There are others who believe these spirits are merely deceitful fantasies that trick those of weak faith; they believe these spirits deceive and frighten inhabitants through deceptive visions and imaginations. These people deny that they are truly spirits because spirits lack a physical body with hands and feet; therefore, they argue, spirits can neither hurt a man nor cause a disturbance. 

Such people are ignorant of the fact that the Angel (who also has a body without hands and feet) carried **Habakkuk** <note>A reference to the story in the deuterocanonical book *Bel and the Dragon*, where an angel carries the prophet Habakkuk by his hair from Judea to Babylon to feed Daniel in the lions' den.</note> along with his entire dinner, by the hair of his head, into **Babylon**, and afterwards brought him back again and set him in his own place. Nor do they consider that the Spirit of the Lord, also without a physical body, snatched up **Philip** and carried him to **Azotus** <note>Refers to Acts 8:39–40, where Philip the Evangelist is miraculously transported after baptizing the Ethiopian eunuch.</note>. 

I shall also mention a certain incorporeal spirit which so disturbed my grandfather’s house that it remained uninhabitable for nearly thirty years—unless a lamp was kept burning within, and even that did not always keep the peace. For when they left the house, the spirit would harass them in the streets by throwing stones from above, even knocking the <term>hearts of pine-trees</term> <note>Original: "hearts of Pine-trees." This refers to highly resinous pine wood, often called "fatwood," which was used as a primitive torch because it burns easily and brightly.</note> right out of their hands as they used them for torches. 

Concerning the ghost that haunted the house of **Athenodorus** <note>Athenodorus Cananites (c. 74 BC – 7 AD), a Stoic philosopher. Pliny the Younger famously recorded a story of him exorcising a ghost in a haunted house in Athens.</note> the Philosopher, and the restless spirit of **C. Caligula** <note>Gaius Caligula (12–41 AD). The Roman historian Suetonius reported that Caligula's ghost haunted the Lamian Gardens where he was hastily buried.</note>, more could be said; but you have already heard their stories in our earlier discussion. From all this, we can easily refute the opinions of those who deny that spirits can walk or move. Now, listen to how much truth there may be in the claims of those who believe these restless spirits are neither devils nor phantoms, but rather the souls of the dead.

*Pollux.* Are there people who hold that opinion?

*Castor.* There are people who hold both opinions: for they declare that these are the souls of those who have departed from their bodies burdened and weighed down by their sins,

<header>A Discourse,</header>

which are therefore heard to be more or less turbulent in houses, according as they have any sensible ardent spark of that fire more or less; so that except in the mean time they are expelled and driven away from thence, or expiated by Alms or Intercessions <note>The text refers to the practice of offering prayers or charity to help the souls of the deceased find rest, a common belief in medieval and early modern theology.</note>, they are compelled to a certain bound of liberty, wandering thereabouts in expectation of the last Judgement.

*Poll.* Wherefore?

<margin>Lactantius on the souls of the dead.</margin>
*Cast.* Because I believe that the souls of them which sleep in Christ, do live with Christ, and do not wander about the earth; and the souls of them who are oppressed and burdened with the grievous weight of their sins, since they are the members of Satan, are bound with Satan in the chains of darkness, expecting judgement in hell.

*Poll.* But *Firmianus* <note>Lucius Caecilius Firmianus Lactantius (c. 250 – c. 325), an early Christian author and advisor to Constantine I.</note>, a Writer of no mean judgement, thinketh the contrary, in his Book which he hath written *On the Divine Reward* <note>original: *de Divino præmio*</note>.

*Cast.* How is that?

<margin>The opinion of Firmianus.</margin>
*Poll.* These are his words: 
> *Let not any man conceive that the souls of the dead are judged immediately after death: for they are all detained in one common custody, until the time shall come, wherein the Almighty Judge shall make examination and inquisition of their deeds. Then they who shall be found righteous, shall receive the reward of immortality; but they whose sins and wickedness shall then be detected, shall not arise again, but shall be inclosed with the wicked in darkness, and destined to eternal punishments.*

*Cast.* *St. Augustine* subscribeth to *Lactantius* in his *Handbook* <note>original: *Enchiridion*. This is Augustine's *Enchiridion on Faith, Hope and Love*, a summary of Christian doctrine written around 420 AD.</note>, saying, 
> *That the time which is interposed between the death of mankinde and the last resurrection, containeth the souls in secret hidden receptacles, whence every soul receiveth condign <note>term: condign (merited or well-deserved)</note> rest or misery, for the good or evil which he did in the body while he lived.*

*Poll.* Neither doth *St. Ambrose* disagree from this: in his second book of *Cain and Abel*, he saith, 
> *that the soul is loosed from the body, and after the end of this life, is suspended to the ambiguous time of the last judgement.*

<header>Of the Nature of Spirits.</header>

*Cast.* So also some have declared, that the soul of Emperor Trajan <note>Trajan (r. 98–117 AD), a Roman Emperor who, according to medieval legend, was temporarily released from the afterlife due to the prayers of Pope Gregory the Great.</note> did wander about; but the soul of *St. George* was freed from such a fate.

*Poll.* Thou hast even now spoke, and that truly, that spacious is the sea of various opinions concerning these Spirits; for so indeed it is: but what port thou touchest at, I desire thee it may not seem troublesome to thee to tell me: for I am not as yet satisfied of the certainty hereof by our discourse.

*Cast.* That which thou desirest, I conceive to be this: I hold that these tumultuous Spirits are mere images of Satan; which are not to be feared, neither is there any credit to be given to their answers: and are in no wise the souls of the dead, which either live with Christ, if they have done well; or else are bound in chains with Satan, if they have done evil.

*Poll.* It remaineth that we sift out this, *Castor*: for it happeneth now sometimes, that my father appeareth to me in my sleep; perhaps that may also seem unto thee to be a Spirit.

*Cast.* It may seem so: but I will not in any thing contradict thee beyond Reason: of myself I will add nothing; but at leastwise I will annihilate thy opinion with the assertions of *St. Augustine*.

*Poll.* What assertions are those?

*Cast.* In his eleventh book, which he entitleth *On Care for the Dead* <note>original: "De mortuorum cura," usually titled *De cura pro mortuis gerenda*.</note>, he offereth them as a means, saying, *Human weakness doth so believe of itself, that when he seeth any one that is dead, in his sleep, he supposeth that he seeth the soul of that dead person; but when he dreameth of any one that is alive, he then is out of doubt, that neither his soul nor his body, but the likeness of the man appeared unto him: As if they could be ignorant, that the souls of dead men do not appear unto them in dreams, but only the likenesses of the persons deceased.* And he proveth both these to be done, by two examples which were at Milan <note>original: "Mediolanus"</note>; whereof the first he sheweth to have been the image of a certain father that was dead, who appeared to his son, admonishing him that he should not pay again a debt to an

<summary>Castor argues that ghosts and apparitions are demonic deceptions or psychological "similitudes" rather than actual souls. He supports this by citing Saint Augustine's view that dreaming of the dead is no different than dreaming of the living—both are mere mental images.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Castor and Pollux regarding the nature of ghosts. Castor is citing examples from St. Augustine’s "De cura pro mortuis gerenda" (On the Care to be Taken for the Dead) to argue that visions of the deceased are not the actual souls of the dead, but rather divinely sent images or angelic interventions.</meta>

# A Discourse,

unjust creditor, whom the father had paid before his death. For Augustine says the case was this: the father had paid a debt to a certain creditor; however, after the father died, the creditor attempted to use force to recover the money again from the son, who did not know the payment had already been made. As the son slept, the image of his father appeared to him and showed him where the written receipt was hidden. When the son woke from his sleep, he searched for the paper in the place he was directed and found it, thereby defeating the malice of his deceitful creditor. 

The second example is one where the same *St. Augustine* shows that the living can appear to the living in their sleep. He tells of *Eulogius* <note>A student and later a teacher of rhetoric in North Africa.</note> the Rhetorician, who was teaching the rhetoric of *Cicero* at *Carthage*. He found a difficult and obscure passage that had not been explained to him, and he vexed himself both waking and sleeping because of his ignorance. But one night, the image of *Aurelius Augustine* appeared to him and taught him how that dark and difficult passage was to be understood.

*Pollux.* Augustine therefore concludes, without a doubt, that these visions are not the actual souls of the dead.

*Castor.* He does indeed conclude that. To further strengthen his judgment, he adds that if the souls of the dead had any power or interest in the affairs of the living, he knew for certain that his own devoted mother <note>St. Monica (c. 332–387 AD), whose relationship with her son Augustine is a central theme in his "Confessions."</note> would not have deserted him. She had followed him by both sea and land while she lived, and he never suffered any heartache without her seeking to comfort his sorrows. 

Lest this seem too harsh a statement, this teacher of Christ <note>original: "praesident of Christ," likely referring to Augustine as a leader in the Church.</note> teaches that those who claim that good Angels—by the appointment of God and divine providence—sometimes visit and come to men, both while they are awake and sleeping, are not in error. These Angels may even visit the places where souls endure punishment. However, this does not happen to everyone, but only to those who have lived in such a way that God judges them worthy of this mercy, or to those whom God, regardless of their own merits, is pleased to glori—

<summary>This page recounts two stories from St. Augustine to prove that "ghosts" are actually mental images or angelic visitations; Augustine argues that if the dead could truly return, his own mother would have visited him in his grief.</summary>

# Of the Nature of Spirits.

...glorify his unspeakable mercy; that by the prayers of the living they may obtain pardon of their sins, and deliverance from the prison of torments <note>A reference to the belief in Purgatory, where the living could assist the souls of the deceased through prayer.</note>.

*Pollux.* I have sometimes read, that the same St. *Augustine* did write, that it is better for a man to doubt of secret things, than to contend about things uncertain.

*Castor.* That is certainly true; neither does he declare himself to be an offense to those who do leave all these things to the unsearchable judgments of God, and labor not to find out the secrets thereof.

<margin>
Whether the devils work Miracles really, or not.
</margin>

*Pollux.* Because I have easily understood your answers hitherto, I will not stop until you have fully resolved me concerning this subject. I desire therefore to know whether all Miracles which the devils perform, are done really, or are merely imaginary phantasies.

*Castor.* That they perform many things really, and many things only seemingly, we have already shown from the Writings of St. *Augustine*. For that great Bishop <note>original: "Prelate"</note> of the Christian Church writes, in the 11th Chapter of his book *On the Trinity* <note>original: "de Trinitate"</note>, *That it is a very easy thing for the wicked Spirits, through the <term>aëry substance</term> <note>Many early theologians believed demons and angels had bodies made of "thick air," which allowed them to take form and interact with the physical world while remaining invisible or shifting.</note> of their bodies, to perform many things which seem wonderful (to the souls that are oppressed with earthly bodies) to be done. He also says, That earthly bodies may be so qualified with art and exercise, that in public Theaters they may perform such wonderful things, that those who never have seen them will not believe them, but that they were done by the assistance of the devil and his ministers, to make their bodies of such an aëry element, that the flesh wonders at. Or else, which is much, he says also, That they do contrive with hidden inspirations, forms, and fantasies of images, to delude human sense; wherewith, waking or sleeping, they may be deceived.* Thus far *Augustine*. But, if you wish, I will produce also another witness beyond reproach, *Pollux*.

*Pollux.* I would have you tell me who that is.

*Castor.* *Abbot Trithemius* <note>Johannes Trithemius (1462–1516), a German Benedictine abbot and a famous writer on cryptography, history, and the occult.</note>, in his third Question to St. *Maximus Emilianus*, which was spoken of before, says thus: *The devils, among unfaithful people, do seem to raise up the dead to*

# A Discourse,

<margin>The devils cannot really raise the dead.</margin>
...life, and to show miracles to curious men, that they might as it were swallow them up with error instead of miracles; and they are altogether stubborn and obstinate. But they cannot truly and really raise up the dead, but do variously deceive the senses of men, showing them feigned resemblances of the dead. For it is certainly manifest, that the devils can do all things, but only in a false imitation of holy miracles in truth.

*Pollux.* Some say that the devils are obedient to wicked men, because of the similarity of their malice. How does that seem to you?

*Castor.* It seems to me, that they are obedient to evil men, but not to all men.

*Pollux.* But to whom?

*Castor.* To those certainly with whom they have contracted and made compacts and covenants; such as those women which they call <term>Pythonists</term> <note>From the Greek 'Python'; a term used in the early modern period to describe soothsayers, mediums, or witches believed to be possessed by a spirit of divination.</note> are accounted, who have vowed themselves by promise unto him <note>Referring to the devil.</note>.

*Pollux.* But although they are compelled to be so serviceable unto them, yet is this service true or feigned?

*Castor.* It is feigned, certainly: for they are subservient unto men of their own accord, and by their own natural work, so that they may deceive them, and allure them to themselves. Although we do not deny that their service is sometimes true, but only towards those men, whose faith in the Lord Jesus Christ, by the merit of his holiness, has caused them to be acceptable, and friends unto him. And **Lactantius** <note>Lucius Caelius Firmianus Lactantius (c. 250 – c. 325), an early Christian author who became an advisor to Emperor Constantine I.</note> also testifies to this, in these words, in the 16th Chapter of his second book, **On the Origin of Error** <note>original: "De origine Erroris"</note>: "That the devils do fear the just, that is, those that worship God, in whose Name they are <term>conjured</term> <note>In this context, to be "conjured" means to be commanded or compelled by a sacred oath or name to depart.</note> to depart out of bodies, and with whose words they are beaten as it were with scourges: and they do not only confess that they are devils, but do declare their names: neither can they lie unto the just." And the same **Lactantius** in the 27th Chapter of his fourth book, **On True Wisdom** <note>original: "De vera Sapientia"</note>, says: "That it is necessary that they who are of the true Christian Religion, should know the course and order of the devils, and understand their subtlety, and restrain their force, and con—"

<image-desc>A rectangular decorative woodcut headpiece featuring elaborate floral scrolls, vines, and two small figures or cherubs entwined in the foliage.</image-desc>

## GERARD OF CREMONA
### REGARDING
# ASTRONOMICAL GEOMANCY.

BEcause Astronomy is such a superior and complex Art <note>The original uses "transcendent and subtil," implying the subject is both beyond ordinary reach and requires great precision.</note> in itself, and because a person must consider so many factors before they can reach a true judgment through it, even a sharp mind cannot grasp half of it. Few scholars <note>original: "Doctors"</note> of our modern time have been found so experienced in it that they know sufficiently how to judge by it; Therefore I have composed this work, which I wish to be named, *Astronomical Geomancy*; in this, I will sufficiently teach how to judge with less labor and study. For in this present science it is not necessary to observe either the Ascendant <note>The "Ascendant" is the specific degree of the Zodiac rising on the eastern horizon at the moment of a reading.</note>, nor the hour in a Table, as is required in *Astrology*.

It is necessary therefore, to make four unequal lines of points set down at random; and to join those points together in pairs; and from the points which are not joined—those remaining at the ends of the lines (as is done in Geomancy <note>Geomancy is a method of divination that interprets patterns of points; here, the author is blending it with astrological signs.</note>)—extract one figure. Take the sign of the Zodiac that corresponds to that figure and set it as the Ascendant, for the sake of the reading. 
<margin>Acquisition,</margin>
If *Acquisitio* <note>original: "Acquisitio" (Latin for "Acquisition" or "Gain"), a geomantic figure associated with expansion and profit, often linked to the planet Jupiter.</note> arises from the ends of those

<summary>This page introduces Gerard of Cremona’s "Astronomical Geomancy," a simplified system of divination designed to bypass the difficult mathematical calculations of traditional astrology. It describes the method of generating a geomantic figure from random points to determine a symbolic starting point for an astrological reading.</summary>

<keywords>Gerard of Cremona, Astronomical Geomancy, astrology vs geomancy, divination methods, Ascendant, Acquisitio, occult science</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for "Astronomical Geomancy," a hybrid system that uses the simple dot-marking techniques of geomancy to generate a traditional twelve-house astrological chart without the need for complex astronomical tables or precise timekeeping.</meta>

->Gerard of Cremona<-

...four lines. <note>The author refers to the four rows of points created during a geomancy reading.</note> If the resulting figure is found, let **Aries** be placed in the **Ascendant** <note>The "Ascendant" is the First House of an astrological chart, representing the starting point of the reading.</note>; if the figure is **Joy** <note>original: "Lætitia"</note> or the **Lesser Fortune** <note>original: "Fortuna Minor"</note>, put **Taurus** in the Ascendant; if **The Boy** <note>original: "Puer"</note> or **The Red** <note>original: "Rubens"</note>, place **Gemini**; if **The White** <note>original: "Albus"</note>, **Cancer**; if **The Way** <note>original: "Via"</note>, **Leo**; if **The Conjunction** <note>original: "Conjunctio"</note> or the **Dragon’s Head** <note>The North Node of the Moon, known in Latin as *Caput Draconis*.</note>, **Virgo**; if **The Girl** <note>original: "Puella"</note>, **Libra**; if **Loss** <note>original: "Amissio"</note> or **Sorrow** <note>original: "Tristitia"</note>, **Scorpio**; if the **Dragon’s Tail** <note>The South Node of the Moon, or *Cauda Draconis*.</note>, **Sagittarius**; if **The People** <note>original: "Populus"</note>, **Capricorn**; if **Greater Fortune** <note>original: "Fortune major"</note>, **Aquarius**; if **The Prison** <note>original: "Carcer"</note>, then put **Pisces** for the Ascendant.

Afterward, in the second House, let that sign be placed which immediately follows the first. In the third House, the third Sign, and so place the rest in order until you come to the end of the Signs. Make one square figure divided into twelve equal parts, and therein place the Signs in order, as is done in **Astrology**, and as you may find them in this figure. In this method, we do not need to consider the **Witnesses** or the **Judge** <note>In traditional geomancy, the "Witnesses" and the "Judge" are the final figures produced to summarize a reading; here, the author discards them in favor of an astrological layout.</note>, or any other thing that belongs to **Geomancy**; we use only the sixteen Figures so that through them we may determine the twelve Signs to which they correspond. Observe the manner of the Figure as it is placed here:

<meta>This page continues the instructions for generating a geomantic-astrological chart. Having previously assigned the zodiac signs to the twelve houses, the author now describes how to determine the positions of the planets within those houses using the "point-pricking" method of geomancy.</meta>

->Of Astronomical Geomancy.<-

V 2
<meta>catchword: mean</meta>

Afterward, it is necessary to make four lines in sequence for every planet by marking down points at random; do the same for the Dragon’s Head <note>The Dragon's Head (Caput Draconis) refers to the North Node of the Moon, a point where the Moon's orbit crosses the ecliptic.</note>, just as you did for the Ascendant. Divide those points by twelve; whatever number remains—or if twelve itself remains—retain that number. Place the planet for which the calculation was made into the House corresponding to that remaining number. That is: if twelve remains, let the planet be placed in the twelfth House; if ten, in the tenth House; if one, in the first House; if two, in the second House; and so on for the rest.

You must always begin with the **Sun**, then the **Moon**, followed by **Venus**, **Mercury**, **Saturn**, **Jupiter**, **Mars**, the **Dragon’s Head**, and the **Dragon’s Tail** <note>The South Node of the Moon.</note>. However, you must always take care not to ask a question during rainy, cloudy, or very windy weather, nor when you are angry or your mind is preoccupied with many affairs. Do not perform this for those who only wish to test or mock you, and do not repeat the same question again using the same figure or form, for that leads to error.

### *Questions of the First House.*

If you wish to know concerning the life of any person—whether it shall be long or short—look to the Lord of the **Ascendant** <note>The "Lord" is the planet that rules the zodiac sign located in the First House (the Ascendant).</note>. If that planet is in strong **Angles** <note>The "Angles" are the 1st, 4th, 7th, and 10th Houses, considered the most powerful positions in a chart.</note>, it signifies a long life; if in **Succedents** <note>The "Succedent" houses are the 2nd, 5th, 8th, and 11th, representing moderate strength.</note>, a middle age; and in **Cadent** <note>The "Cadent" houses are the 3rd, 6th, 9th, and 12th, considered the weakest positions.</note> Houses, a short life. Furthermore, if the planet is in strong Angles, it signifies the "greater years"; if in Succedents, the "mean years"; and if in Cadents, the "lesser years."

*   The lesser years of **Saturn** are thirty, the mean are forty-four years, and the greater are fifty-eight.
*   The lesser years of **Jupiter** are twelve, the mean years are forty, and the greater accordingly are forty-seven.
*   The lesser years of **Mars** are fifteen, his mean years are forty, and the greater years are forty-seven.
*   The lesser years of the **Sun** are nineteen, his mean years are forty-five, and his greater years are eighty-two.
*   The lesser years of **Venus** are eight, her mean years are forty-five, and her greater years are eighty-two.
*   **Mercury’s** lesser years are twenty, his...

<meta>This page transitions from the descriptions of social classes and professions associated with planets to the practical application of astrology for specific inquiries.
